## Q1

Chemistry · organic · Hydrocarbons

2,3-Dimethyl-2-butene can be prepared by heating which of the following compounds with a strong acid?

- (1) $\mathrm{(CH_3)_2C=CH-CH_2-CH_3}$
- (2) $\mathrm{(CH_3)_2CH-CH_2-CH=CH_2}$
- (3) $\mathrm{(CH_3)_2CH-CH(CH_3)-CH=CH_2}$
- **(4)** $\mathrm{(CH_3)_3C-CH=CH_2}$

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: 3,3-dimethylbut-1-ene →(H⁺) 3,3-dimethylbutan-2-ylium; 3,3-dimethylbutan-2-ylium →(−C̄H₃) 2,3-dimethylbutan-2-ylium; 2,3-dimethylbutan-2-ylium →(−H⁺) 2,3-dimethylbut-2-ene (2,3-Dimethyl-2-butene)](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q001_sol_1_11031cdb_28102026.png?v=11031cdb123a)

*3,3-dimethylbut-1-ene →[H⁺] 3,3-dimethylbutan-2-ylium; 3,3-dimethylbutan-2-ylium →[−C̄H₃] 2,3-dimethylbutan-2-ylium; 2,3-dimethylbutan-2-ylium →[−H⁺] 2,3-dimethylbut-2-ene (2,3-Dimethyl-2-butene)*


**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The target $\mathrm{(CH_3)_2C=C(CH_3)_2}$ has 6 carbons with a $\mathrm{C_4}$ chain carrying two methyls; option 3 has 7 carbons and options 1 and 2 have unbranched skeletons that cannot give it without a rearrangement of the right type.
2. Protonation of option 4 by Markovnikov addition: $\mathrm{(CH_3)_3C-CH=CH_2 + H^+ \rightarrow (CH_3)_3C-\overset{+}{C}H-CH_3}$ (secondary carbocation).
3. A 1,2-methyl shift gives the more stable tertiary carbocation: $\mathrm{(CH_3)_3C-\overset{+}{C}H-CH_3 \rightarrow (CH_3)_2\overset{+}{C}-CH(CH_3)_2}$.
4. Loss of $\mathrm{H^+}$ from the adjacent C–H gives the most substituted alkene: $\mathrm{(CH_3)_2\overset{+}{C}-CH(CH_3)_2 \rightarrow (CH_3)_2C=C(CH_3)_2}$, i.e. 2,3-dimethyl-2-butene, which is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Preparation of Alkenes · **Difficulty:** hard (4 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 1)

![printed question 1](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q001_84156321_28102026.png?v=84156321a0b8)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 1](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q001_dd57c230_28102026.png?v=dd57c23028e6)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Reaction outcome** (`reaction`) — what a reaction gives, or the reagent it needs
- **Concept:** Acid-catalysed alkene isomerisation: protonate, let a 1,2-methyl shift build the most stable carbocation, then lose a proton - so the starting material must already carry the product's carbon skeleton
- **Trap:** **Condition dropped** (`condition_missed`) — drop the condition the stem states and the option becomes true — option 1  
  A student holding 'strong acid moves the double bond to the more substituted carbon' sees option 1 already carrying an internal, branched $\mathrm{C=C}$ and takes it as one nudge away from the tetrasubstituted target. Moving the double bond inside 2-methyl-2-pentene cannot build the 2,3-dimethylbutane skeleton the product needs.
- **Why:** This question requires the starting material a named reagent converts to a named product, demonstrated by 'can be prepared by heating which of the following compounds with a strong acid'.
- **Note:** Option 3 as printed is $\mathrm{C_7H_{14}}$ and so is not an isomer of the $\mathrm{C_6H_{12}}$ product; tagged as printed, and the key (4) is unaffected by it.

## Q2

Chemistry · inorganic · The D- and F-Block Elements – Practice

Gadolinium belongs of 4f series. Its atomic number is 64. Which of the following is the correct electronic configuration of gadolinium?

- **(1)** $\mathrm{[Xe]4f^{7}5d^{1}6s^{2}}$
- (2) $\mathrm{[Xe]4f^{6}5d^{2}6s^{2}}$
- (3) $\mathrm{[Xe]4f^{8}6d^{2}}$
- (4) $\mathrm{[Xe]4f^{9}5s^{1}}$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. ${}_{64}\mathrm{Gd} = {}_{54}[\mathrm{Xe}]6s^{2}\,4f^{7}\,5d^{1}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. $Z = 64 = 54\,(\mathrm{Xe}) + 10$, so $10$ electrons go beyond the $[\mathrm{Xe}]$ core.
2. $6s$ fills first: $6s^{2}$, leaving $8$ electrons.
3. A half-filled $4f^{7}$ subshell is extra stable, so $7$ go into $4f$ and the last one into $5d$: $4f^{7}5d^{1}$.
4. Configuration $[\mathrm{Xe}]4f^{7}5d^{1}6s^{2}$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Electronic Configurations of the d-Block & f-Block · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 1)

![printed question 2](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q002_144e6583_28102026.png?v=144e6583df56)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 2](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q002_d343e44b_28102026.png?v=d343e44b3bb4)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** Writing a lanthanoid configuration: 6s fills before 4f and 5d, and gadolinium promotes exactly one 4f electron to 5d so that 4f stays half-filled
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 2  
  A student who stores the gadolinium exception as 'some 4f electrons move across into 5d' splits the eight electrons left after $\mathrm{6s^2}$ as $\mathrm{4f^6 5d^2}$, and so throws away the half-filled $\mathrm{4f^7}$ that is the only thing the exception buys.
- **Why:** This question requires the filling order and the half-filled-shell exception applied to a given atomic number, demonstrated by 'Its atomic number is 64' standing in the stem while all four options already total 64 electrons.
- **Note:** All four options total 64 electrons, so counting electrons strikes none of them out; only the orbital order and the half-filled exception decide between them.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q3

Chemistry · inorganic · Classification of Elements and Periodicity in Properties

The formation of the oxide ion, $\mathrm{O^{2-}(g)}$, from oxygen atom requires first an exothermic and then an endothermic step as shown in below:
$\mathrm{O(g) + e^{-} \longrightarrow O^{-}(g)}\;;\;\Delta_{f}H^{\ominus} = -141\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$
$\mathrm{O^{-}(g) + e^{-} \longrightarrow O^{2-}(g)}\;;\;\Delta_{f}H^{\ominus} = +780\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$
Thus process of formation of $\mathrm{O^{2-}}$ in gas phase is unfavourable though $\mathrm{O^{2-}}$ is isoelectronic with neon. It is due to the fact that,

- (1) Oxygen is more electronegative.
- (2) Addition of electron in oxygen results in larger size of the ion.
- **(3)** Electron repulsion outweighs the stability gained by achieving noble gas configuration.
- (4) $\mathrm{O^{-}}$ ion has comparatively smaller size than oxygen atom.

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Net enthalpy for $\mathrm{O(g) + 2e^{-} \rightarrow O^{2-}(g)}$: $\Delta H = -141 + 780 = +639\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$, so the overall process is endothermic.
2. The second electron is added to an already negative $\mathrm{O^{-}}$ ion, so it faces strong electron-electron repulsion; this repulsion outweighs the stability gained from the neon-like $\mathrm{O^{2-}}$ configuration, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Trends in Chemical Properties – Electron Gain Enthalpy · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 1)

![printed question 3](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q003_838c9ed2_28102026.png?v=838c9ed29f1f)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 3](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q003_2adf2a56_28102026.png?v=2adf2a56c210)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Stated reason** (`reason_line`) — a reason NCERT gives: why it happens
- **Concept:** Why the second electron gain is endothermic: the incoming electron has to be forced onto an ion that is already negative, and that repulsion costs more than the noble-gas configuration returns
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 4  
  A student who has the mechanism right - the incoming electron is crowded by the charge already there - reaches for 'smaller size' as the way to say crowded, but $\mathrm{O^-}$ is larger than O, so the option states the size order upside down.
- **Why:** This question requires the reason behind two enthalpies the stem has already supplied, demonstrated by the stem ending 'It is due to the fact that,'.
- **Note:** E1's pattern read this as the retired `statement_options`. No lettered statement block is printed and the stem is a trailing 'due to the fact that', so under the rule that full-sentence options alone do not make a question `statements`, the qtype recorded is `mcq`.

## Q4

Chemistry · organic · Amines

The number of structural isomers possible from the molecular formula $\mathrm{C_3H_9N}$ is:

- (1) 2
- (2) 3
- **(3)** 4
- (4) 5

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: $\ce{C3H9N}$: propan-1-amine ($\ce{CH3-CH2-CH2-NH2}$); propan-2-amine ($\ce{CH3-CH(NH2)-CH3}$) — 1° amine](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q004_sol_1_20f98730_28102026.png?v=20f987305032)

*$\ce{C3H9N}$: propan-1-amine ($\ce{CH3-CH2-CH2-NH2}$); propan-2-amine ($\ce{CH3-CH(NH2)-CH3}$) — 1° amine*

1. $\mathrm{CH_3-CH_2-NH-CH_3}$ $\}$ $2^{\circ}$ amine

![printed figure: N,N-dimethylmethanamine ($\ce{(CH3)3N}$) — 3° amine](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q004_sol_2_bddced13_28102026.png?v=bddced134af9)

*N,N-dimethylmethanamine ($\ce{(CH3)3N}$) — 3° amine*


**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. $\mathrm{C_3H_9N}$ is saturated (degree of unsaturation $= \frac{2(3)+2+1-9}{2} = 0$), so only open-chain amines are possible.
2. Primary amines: $\mathrm{CH_3CH_2CH_2NH_2}$ (propan-1-amine) and $\mathrm{(CH_3)_2CHNH_2}$ (propan-2-amine), giving $2$.
3. Secondary amine: $\mathrm{CH_3CH_2NHCH_3}$ (N-methylethanamine), giving $1$.
4. Tertiary amine: $\mathrm{(CH_3)_3N}$ (trimethylamine), giving $1$.
5. Total $= 2 + 1 + 1 = 4$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Structures, Nomenclature and Classification of Amines · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 1)

![printed question 4](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q004_b305d9e0_28102026.png?v=b305d9e0c065)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 4](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q004_dfb5d05b_28102026.png?v=dfb5d05be4b5)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Counting structural isomers of $\mathrm{C_3H_9N}$: walk the nitrogen through every position it can take - two primary amines, one secondary, one tertiary
- **Trap:** **Arithmetic slip** (`arithmetic_slip`) — the number or the unit is wrong, or a step was stopped short — option 2  
  Three is what the tally gives when one structure is dropped, and two different drops both land on it: counting one amine per class misses the second primary isomer, and drawing the two propylamines plus trimethylamine misses the secondary amine.
- **Why:** This question requires a count the student generates by enumerating every structure the formula allows, demonstrated by the options being the bare integers 2, 3, 4 and 5 with nothing supplied but 'the molecular formula $\mathrm{C_3H_9N}$'.
- **Note:** `calculate` is recorded for a tally the student produces by enumeration rather than by arithmetic on printed numbers; no value on the `asks` axis is specific to enumeration, and `apply_rule` was ruled out because a quantity is produced.

## Q5

Chemistry · physical · Chemical Equilibrium

If the equilibrium constant for $\mathrm{N_2(g) + O_2(g) \rightleftharpoons 2NO(g)}$ is K, the equilibrium constant for $\mathrm{\frac{1}{2}N_2(g) + \frac{1}{2}O_2(g) \rightleftharpoons NO(g)}$ will be:

- (1) $\mathrm{K}$
- (2) $\mathrm{K^{2}}$
- **(3)** $\mathrm{K^{\frac{1}{2}}}$
- (4) $\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{K}$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mathrm{N_2(g) + O_2(g) \rightleftharpoons 2NO(g)}$ ; $\mathrm{K}$
2. $\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{N_2(g)} + \frac{1}{2}\mathrm{O_2(g) \rightleftharpoons NO(g)}$ ; $\mathrm{K'}$
3. $\mathrm{K} = \frac{[\mathrm{NO}]^{2}}{[\mathrm{N_2}]\;[\mathrm{O_2}]}$
4. $\mathrm{K'} = \frac{\mathrm{NO}}{[\mathrm{N_2}]^{1/2}[\mathrm{O_2}]^{1/2}}$
5. $\therefore \mathrm{K'} = \sqrt{\mathrm{K}}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. For $\mathrm{N_2 + O_2 \rightleftharpoons 2NO}$, $K = \frac{[\mathrm{NO}]^2}{[\mathrm{N_2}][\mathrm{O_2}]}$.
2. For $\mathrm{\frac{1}{2}N_2 + \frac{1}{2}O_2 \rightleftharpoons NO}$, $K' = \frac{[\mathrm{NO}]}{[\mathrm{N_2}]^{1/2}[\mathrm{O_2}]^{1/2}}$.
3. $K' = \left(\frac{[\mathrm{NO}]^2}{[\mathrm{N_2}][\mathrm{O_2}]}\right)^{1/2} = K^{1/2}$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Law of Chemical Equilibrium · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 2)

![printed question 5](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q005_1c25e365_28102026.png?v=1c25e365d8ff)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 5](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q005_46c540af_28102026.png?v=46c540af2ad3)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** The equilibrium constant follows the balanced equation it was written for: multiply the equation by $n$ and $K$ is raised to the power $n$, so halving the equation gives $K^{1/2}$
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 4  
  A student carries the scaling habit of $\Delta H$ - halve the equation, halve the quantity - across to the equilibrium constant and writes $\frac{1}{2}K$. The coefficients are exponents in the mass-action expression, so halving them takes a square root rather than a half.
- **Why:** This question requires the constant of a rescaled equation, got from the mass-action expression, demonstrated by the options being powers and multiples of $K$ for '$\frac{1}{2}N_2(g) + \frac{1}{2}O_2(g) \rightleftharpoons NO(g)$'.
- **Note:** `relation` was ruled out: it is reserved for how many times one quantity changes when another does, and the answer here is a power of $K$ derived from the two mass-action expressions, which the axis sends to `calculate` even though the result is symbolic.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q6

Chemistry · physical · Ionic Equilibrium

Which one of the following pairs of solution is not an acidic buffer?

- (1) $\mathrm{H_2CO_3}$ and $\mathrm{Na_2CO_3}$
- (2) $\mathrm{H_3PO_4}$ and $\mathrm{Na_3PO_4}$
- **(3)** $\mathrm{HClO_4}$ and $\mathrm{NaClO_4}$
- (4) $\mathrm{CH_3COOH}$ and $\mathrm{CH_3COONa}$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Strong acid with its salt cannot form buffer solution.
2. $\mathrm{HClO_4}$ and $\mathrm{NaClO_4}$ cannot act as an acidic buffer.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. An acidic buffer is a weak acid together with its salt with a strong base, e.g. $\mathrm{CH_3COOH}/\mathrm{CH_3COONa}$.
2. $\mathrm{H_2CO_3}$ and $\mathrm{H_3PO_4}$ are weak acids, so options $1$, $2$ and $4$ are weak acid + salt pairs.
3. $\mathrm{HClO_4}$ is a strong acid and ionises completely, so $\mathrm{HClO_4}/\mathrm{NaClO_4}$ cannot resist pH change: it is not a buffer, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Buffer Solutions · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 2)

![printed question 6](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q006_f0ce3ffb_28102026.png?v=f0ce3ffbb1d6)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 6](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q006_424b6b2e_28102026.png?v=424b6b2edd86)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Two answers** (`two_part`) — the answer has two halves, usually joined by 'respectively'
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** An acidic buffer needs a weak acid together with the salt of that same acid; a strong acid is completely ionised, so it and its salt hold no reserve to buffer with
- **Trap:** **Answers another question** (`answers_other_question`) — true, and about this topic, but it answers something else — option 4  
  Acetic acid with sodium acetate is the acidic buffer the chapter is built around, so a student reading the stem as 'which pair IS an acidic buffer' lands on the pair they can name. It is a genuine acidic buffer, which is exactly what disqualifies it from a stem carrying 'not'.
- **Why:** This question requires the buffer criterion applied to four acid-and-salt pairs, demonstrated by the one failing pair being '$\mathrm{HClO_4}$ and $\mathrm{NaClO_4}$', whose acid is strong.
- **Note:** E1's pattern returned `plain_mcq`, but each option is a pair of solutions accepted or rejected whole, which is the rulebook's §1 `pair_options` shape; `two_part` is the nearest value on the current qtype axis and is recorded in place of `mcq`. The stem's task carries 'not'.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q7

Chemistry · physical · Electrochemistry

Aqueous solution of which of the following compounds is the best conductor of electric current?

- (1) Ammonia, $\mathrm{NH_3}$
- (2) Fructose, $\mathrm{C_6H_{12}O_6}$
- (3) Acetic acid, $\mathrm{C_2H_4O_2}$
- **(4)** Hydrochloric acid, $\mathrm{HCl}$

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Aqueous solution of $\mathrm{HCl}$ is the best conductor of electric current because $\mathrm{HCl}$ is strong acid, so it dissociates completely into ions.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Fructose, $\mathrm{C_6H_{12}O_6}$, is a non-electrolyte and gives no ions in water.
2. $\mathrm{NH_3}$ and acetic acid $\mathrm{CH_3COOH}$ are weak electrolytes and ionise only partially.
3. $\mathrm{HCl} \rightarrow \mathrm{H^+} + \mathrm{Cl^-}$ ionises completely, so its solution has the most ions and conducts best: option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Weak Electrolytes · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 2)

![printed question 7](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q007_14b33e0f_28102026.png?v=14b33e0fa026)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 7](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q007_c1c82526_28102026.png?v=c1c825267099)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Comparison** (`compare`) — the difference between two things, or the correct pairing
- **Concept:** Conductivity of a solution is set by how many ions the solute actually furnishes: a strong electrolyte dissociates almost completely, a weak one only partly, a non-electrolyte not at all
- **Trap:** **Neighbouring fact** (`nearby_fact`) — a true fact about a neighbouring thing is offered instead — option 3  
  Acetic acid is the acid NCERT prints beside HCl when it contrasts weak electrolytes with strong ones, so a student who knows acids conduct reaches for the acid they have just seen conducting. Acetic acid ionises only slightly, so it supplies far fewer ions than HCl, which dissociates almost completely.
- **Why:** This question requires the four solutes to be sorted by how completely they ionise in water, demonstrated by the superlative 'best conductor of electric current' standing over a sugar, a weak base, a weak acid and a strong acid.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q8

Chemistry · organic · Polymers

Caprolactum is used for the manufacture of:

- (1) Terylene
- (2) Nylon-6,6
- **(3)** Nylon-6
- (4) Teflon

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: Caprolactum: caprolactam with H–OH (water) drawn across the N–C=O bond, dashed line marking the cleavage → nH( 6-aminohexanoic acid )z; ↓ Nylon-6: (–NH–(CH₂)₅–CO–)n](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q008_sol_1_3c3681da_28102026.png?v=3c3681da51f0)

*Caprolactum: caprolactam with H–OH (water) drawn across the N–C=O bond, dashed line marking the cleavage → nH[ 6-aminohexanoic acid ]z; ↓ Nylon-6: [–NH–(CH₂)₅–CO–]n*


**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Caprolactam is a cyclic amide, $\mathrm{C_6H_{11}NO}$, with six carbon atoms; heating it with water at high temperature opens the ring and it polymerises to $[-\mathrm{NH}-(\mathrm{CH_2})_5-\mathrm{CO}-]_n$, which is Nylon-6, option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Condensation Polymers: Polyamides · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 3)

![printed question 8](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q008_93a78d44_28102026.png?v=93a78d44ecd3)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 8](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q008_98eb6a8f_28102026.png?v=98eb6a8ff20a)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Stated fact** (`fact_line`) — a fact NCERT states: what, who or where
- **Concept:** Caprolactam ring-opens and polymerises on its own to give Nylon-6, whereas Nylon-6,6 is built from two monomers, hexamethylenediamine and adipic acid
- **Trap:** **look alike term** (`look_alike_term`) — two terms RESEMBLE each other and the resemblance is the pull: maturation / meristematic, lyase / ligase, GPP / NPP — option 2  
  Caprolactam is held in memory as 'the nylon monomer', and the two nylon names differ only by a repeated 6, so the whole answer rests on a digit. Nylon-6,6 needs two different monomers; caprolactam alone gives Nylon-6.
- **Why:** This question requires the stated monomer-to-polymer pairing, demonstrated by the stem 'Caprolactum is used for the manufacture of' naming one monomer while the four options name four polymers.
- **Note:** The stem prints 'Caprolactum'; copied as printed, the compound being caprolactam.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q9

Chemistry · inorganic · The s-Block Elements

On heating which of the following releases $\mathrm{CO_2}$ most easily?

- **(1)** $\mathrm{MgCO_3}$
- (2) $\mathrm{CaCO_3}$
- (3) $\mathrm{K_2CO_3}$
- (4) $\mathrm{Na_2CO_3}$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Thermal stability order
2. $\mathrm{K_2CO_3} > \mathrm{Na_2CO_3} > \mathrm{CaCO_3} > \mathrm{MgCO_3}$
3. Therefore $\mathrm{MgCO_3}$ releases $\mathrm{CO_2}$ most easily.
4. $\mathrm{MgCO_3} \xrightarrow{\Delta} \mathrm{MgO} + \mathrm{CO_2}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Alkali metal carbonates ($\mathrm{Na_2CO_3}$, $\mathrm{K_2CO_3}$) are thermally very stable and do not readily lose $\mathrm{CO_2}$ on heating.
2. Group 2 carbonates decompose as $\mathrm{MCO_3 \rightarrow MO + CO_2}$, and their stability increases down the group as cation size increases and polarising power falls.
3. $\mathrm{Mg^{2+}}$ is the smallest, most polarising cation, so the order of stability is $\mathrm{MgCO_3} < \mathrm{CaCO_3} < \mathrm{Na_2CO_3} < \mathrm{K_2CO_3}$.
4. The least stable carbonate, $\mathrm{MgCO_3}$, releases $\mathrm{CO_2}$ most easily, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Alkaline Earth Metals: Properties and Trends · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 3)

![printed question 9](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q009_02f2d91e_28102026.png?v=02f2d91e678a)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 9](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q009_6a666393_28102026.png?v=6a66639315b3)

---

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Comparison** (`compare`) — the difference between two things, or the correct pairing
- **Concept:** Thermal stability of a carbonate rises with cation size: a small, strongly polarising cation pulls at the carbonate ion and weakens it, so that carbonate gives up $\mathrm{CO_2}$ at the lowest temperature
- **Trap:** **Neighbouring fact** (`nearby_fact`) — a true fact about a neighbouring thing is offered instead — option 2  
  $\mathrm{CaCO_3 \to CaO + CO_2}$ is the carbonate decomposition the chapter actually writes an equation for, so a student who knows alkaline-earth carbonates decompose reaches for the one printed decomposing. Inside group 2 stability rises with cation size, so the smaller, more polarising $\mathrm{Mg^{2+}}$ makes $\mathrm{MgCO_3}$ go first.
- **Why:** This question requires the cation-size trend in carbonate thermal stability to be read across two groups, demonstrated by 'releases $\mathrm{CO_2}$ most easily' standing over two alkaline-earth and two alkali carbonates.

## Q10

Chemistry · inorganic · The p–Block Elements

Strong reducing behaviour of $\mathrm{H_3PO_2}$ is due to:

- (1) High oxidation state of phosphorus
- (2) Presence of two $-\mathrm{OH}$ groups and one p – H bond
- **(3)** Presence of one $-\mathrm{OH}$ group and two p – H bonds
- (4) High electron gain enthalpy of phosphorus

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Strong reducing behaviour of $\mathrm{H_3PO_2}$
2. All oxy-acid of phosphorus which contain $\mathrm{P-H}$ bond act as reductant.

![printed figure: phosphinic acid ($\ce{H3PO2}$): P bonded to =O, -OH and two H](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q010_sol_1_0fe7fa66_28102026.png?v=0fe7fa663dc2)

*phosphinic acid ($\ce{H3PO2}$): P bonded to =O, -OH and two H*

3. Presence of one $-\mathrm{OH}$ group and two $\mathrm{P-H}$ bonds.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. $\mathrm{H_3PO_2}$ has the structure $\mathrm{H_2P(=O)OH}$: one $-\mathrm{OH}$ group and two $\mathrm{P{-}H}$ bonds.
2. Oxoacids of phosphorus that contain $\mathrm{P{-}H}$ bonds act as reducing agents, so the two $\mathrm{P{-}H}$ bonds account for its strong reducing behaviour, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Important Compounds and Oxyacids of Phosphorus · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 3)

![printed question 10](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q010_bdbf3422_28102026.png?v=bdbf3422d459)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 10](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q010_0966546c_28102026.png?v=0966546c1269)

- **Asks:** **Stated reason** (`reason_line`) — a reason NCERT gives: why it happens
- **Concept:** In the oxyacids of phosphorus the $-\mathrm{OH}$ hydrogens are the acidic ones and the P–H hydrogens are the reducing ones, so more P–H bonds means a stronger reducing agent
- **Trap:** **Neighbouring fact** (`nearby_fact`) — a true fact about a neighbouring thing is offered instead — option 2  
  P–H bonds are the right handle on reducing behaviour, but 'two $-\mathrm{OH}$ and one P–H' are the counts for $\mathrm{H_3PO_3}$, the oxyacid printed in the row next to $\mathrm{H_3PO_2}$. $\mathrm{H_3PO_2}$ has one $-\mathrm{OH}$ and two P–H bonds, which is why it reduces more strongly than $\mathrm{H_3PO_3}$.

## Q11

Chemistry · inorganic · Chemical Bonding and Molecular Structure

Decreasing order of stability of $\mathrm{O_2}$, $\mathrm{O_2^{-}}$, $\mathrm{O_2^{+}}$ and $\mathrm{O_2^{2-}}$ is:

- (1) $\mathrm{O_2} > \mathrm{O_2^{+}} > \mathrm{O_2^{2-}} > \mathrm{O_2^{-}}$
- (2) $\mathrm{O_2^{-}} > \mathrm{O_2^{2-}} > \mathrm{O_2^{+}} > \mathrm{O_2}$
- **(3)** $\mathrm{O_2^{+}} > \mathrm{O_2} > \mathrm{O_2^{-}} > \mathrm{O_2^{2-}}$
- (4) $\mathrm{O_2^{2-}} > \mathrm{O_2^{-}} > \mathrm{O_2} > \mathrm{O_2^{+}}$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Given species: $\mathrm{O_2}, \mathrm{O_2^{-}}, \mathrm{O_2^{+}}, \mathrm{O_2^{2-}}$
2. Total number of electrons
3. $\mathrm{O_2} \longrightarrow 16e^{-}$
4. $\mathrm{O_2^{-}} \longrightarrow 17e^{-}$
5. $\mathrm{O_2^{+}} \longrightarrow 15e^{-}$
6. $\mathrm{O_2^{2-}} \longrightarrow 18e^{-}$
7. Bond order: $\mathrm{O_2^{+}}$ $2.5$, $\mathrm{O_2}$ $2$, $\mathrm{O_2^{-}}$ $1.5$, $\mathrm{O_2^{2-}}$ $1$
8. Stability $\times$ Bond order
9. Stability order $[\mathrm{O_2^{+}} > \mathrm{O_2} > \mathrm{O_2^{-}} > \mathrm{O_2^{2-}}]$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Stability increases with bond order, $\text{B.O.} = \frac{N_b - N_a}{2}$.
2. $\mathrm{O_2^{+}}$ (15 electrons): $\text{B.O.} = \frac{10-5}{2} = 2.5$.
3. $\mathrm{O_2}$ (16 electrons): $\text{B.O.} = \frac{10-6}{2} = 2$.
4. $\mathrm{O_2^{-}}$ (17 electrons): $\text{B.O.} = \frac{10-7}{2} = 1.5$.
5. $\mathrm{O_2^{2-}}$ (18 electrons): $\text{B.O.} = \frac{10-8}{2} = 1$.
6. So $\mathrm{O_2^{+}} > \mathrm{O_2} > \mathrm{O_2^{-}} > \mathrm{O_2^{2-}}$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Molecular Orbital Theory · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 3)

![printed question 11](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q011_da41081a_28102026.png?v=da41081ac845)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 11](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q011_5ef7ca89_28102026.png?v=5ef7ca891f94)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Sequence** (`order`) — a sequence to put in the right order, or a trend to arrange
- **Concept:** Stability of $\mathrm{O_2}$ and its ions follows molecular-orbital bond order, and every electron added to the antibonding $\pi^*$ orbitals cuts the bond order by a half
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 4  
  Bond order runs 2.5, 2.0, 1.5, 1.0 from $\mathrm{O_2^{+}}$ to $\mathrm{O_2^{2-}}$, and this option is that same list written the other way round — which is where a student lands by ranking the species by charge, or by writing the sequence as an increasing one.
- **Why:** This question requires the four species to be ranked by molecular-orbital bond order, demonstrated by 'Decreasing order of stability' with four complete sequences as options.
- **Note:** E1's pattern called this arrange_in_order, a retired value with no counterpart on the current qtype axis; the printed shape is a plain four-option MCQ whose answers are sequences, so the ordering is carried by asks: order. The stored solution line is truncated at 'Given species'.

## Q12

Chemistry · physical · Basic Concepts of Chemistry

The number of water molecules is maximum in:

- (1) $18\,\text{gram}$ of water
- **(2)** $18\,\text{moles}$ of water
- (3) 18 molecules of water
- (4) $1.8\,\text{gram}$ of water

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\because$ 1 mole water $= 6.02 \times 10^{23}$ moleucles
2. $\therefore$ 18 mole water $= 18 \times 6.02 \times 10^{23}$ molecules
3. So, 18 mole water has maximum number of molecules.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Molar mass of $\text{H}_2\text{O}$ is $18\,\text{g mol}^{-1}$, so $18\,\text{g} = 1\,\text{mol}$, giving $N_A = 6.022\times10^{23}$ molecules.
2. $18\,\text{mol}$ gives $18 N_A = 18 \times 6.022\times10^{23} \approx 1.08\times10^{25}$ molecules.
3. $1.8\,\text{g} = 0.1\,\text{mol}$ gives $0.1 N_A = 6.022\times10^{22}$ molecules; option 3 is only $18$ molecules.
4. The largest count is $18 N_A$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Mole Concept · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 4)

![printed question 12](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q012_9190047e_28102026.png?v=9190047e3d53)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 12](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q012_c97247c2_28102026.png?v=c97247c27b33)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Number of molecules equals moles times Avogadro's number, so amounts stated in grams, moles and molecules must all be converted to one currency before they can be compared
- **Trap:** **Answers another question** (`answers_other_question`) — true, and about this topic, but it answers something else — option 1  
  18 is water's molar mass, so this option is the one the chapter's worked line is about: $18\,\mathrm{g}$ of water is one mole, $6.02\times10^{23}$ molecules. That answers 'which of these is one mole', not 'which is maximum' — 18 moles holds $1.08\times10^{25}$ molecules, eighteen times more.
- **Why:** This question requires the four stated amounts to be converted into molecule counts before they are compared, demonstrated by options printing 18 in three different units — '$18\,\text{gram}$', '$18\,\text{moles}$', '18 molecules'.

## Q13

Chemistry · inorganic · Chemical Bonding and Molecular Structure

In which of the following pairs, both the species are not isostructural?

- (1) $\mathrm{NH_3}, \mathrm{PH_3}$
- **(2)** $\mathrm{XeF_4}, \mathrm{XeO_4}$
- (3) $\mathrm{SiCl_4}, \mathrm{PCl_4^+}$
- (4) Diamond, silicon carbide

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. i. Hybridization of $\mathrm{NH_3}$ $[\sigma = 3, \mathrm{lp} = 1]$
2. $\mathrm{sp^3}$ geometry: Tetrahedral

![printed figure: ammonia, $\ce{NH3}$ (pyramidal, one lone pair on N); phosphine, $\ce{PH3}$ (pyramidal, one lone pair on P)](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q013_sol_1_7c82d9aa_29102026.png?v=7c82d9aa9762)

*ammonia, $\ce{NH3}$ (pyramidal, one lone pair on N); phosphine, $\ce{PH3}$ (pyramidal, one lone pair on P)*

3. ii. Structures of $\mathrm{XeF_4}$ is square planar.

![printed figure: xenon tetrafluoride, $\ce{XeF4}$, with two lone pairs on Xe (square planar), sp3 d2 hybridisation](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q013_sol_2_599ae8fd_29102026.png?v=599ae8fdf6a8)

*xenon tetrafluoride, $\ce{XeF4}$, with two lone pairs on Xe (square planar), sp3 d2 hybridisation*

4. Structure of $\mathrm{XeO_4}$ is tetrahedral

![printed figure: xenon tetroxide, $\ce{XeO4}$, sp3 hybridisation](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q013_sol_3_0b72ab21_29102026.png?v=0b72ab2125a7)

*xenon tetroxide, $\ce{XeO4}$, sp3 hybridisation*

5. So $\mathrm{XeF_4}$ and $\mathrm{XeO_4}$ are not isostructural.
6. iii. Structure of $\mathrm{SiCl_4}$ is tetrahedral.

![printed figure: silicon tetrachloride, $\ce{SiCl4}$, sp3 hybridisation](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q013_sol_4_f629168e_29102026.png?v=f629168e9546)

*silicon tetrachloride, $\ce{SiCl4}$, sp3 hybridisation*

7. Structure of $\mathrm{PCl_4^+}$ is tetrahedral.

![printed figure: tetrachlorophosphonium ion, $\ce{PCl4+}$, sp3 hybridisation](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/29102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q013_sol_5_92cc9d30_29102026.png?v=92cc9d30360c)

*tetrachlorophosphonium ion, $\ce{PCl4+}$, sp3 hybridisation*


**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. $\mathrm{NH_3}$ and $\mathrm{PH_3}$ both have 3 bond pairs and 1 lone pair, so both are trigonal pyramidal: isostructural.
2. $\mathrm{XeF_4}$ has 4 bond pairs and 2 lone pairs on Xe ($sp^3d^2$), so it is square planar.
3. $\mathrm{XeO_4}$ has 4 bonding domains and no lone pair on Xe ($sp^3$), so it is tetrahedral; hence $\mathrm{XeF_4}$ and $\mathrm{XeO_4}$ are not isostructural.
4. $\mathrm{SiCl_4}$ and $\mathrm{PCl_4^+}$ both have 4 bond pairs and no lone pair ($sp^3$), so both are tetrahedral: isostructural.
5. Diamond and silicon carbide are both 3D networks of tetrahedrally bonded $sp^3$ atoms: isostructural.
6. The only non-isostructural pair is $\mathrm{XeF_4}, \mathrm{XeO_4}$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Valence Shell Electron Pair Repulsion (VSEPR) Theory · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 4)

![printed question 13](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q013_c9df1834_28102026.png?v=c9df18341e8d)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 13](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q013_16169168_28102026.png?v=16169168c664)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Two answers** (`two_part`) — the answer has two halves, usually joined by 'respectively'
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** Isostructural means the same VSEPR shape: count bond pairs and lone pairs on the central atom, not bond angles
- **Trap:** **Answers another question** (`answers_other_question`) — true, and about this topic, but it answers something else — option 1  
  $\mathrm{NH_3}$ and $\mathrm{PH_3}$ are the pair the textbook contrasts for bond angle, $107^\circ$ against about $94^\circ$, so that stored contrast is read as 'these two differ'; the angles do differ, but both molecules are trigonal pyramidal, which is what isostructural asks about.
- **Why:** This question requires VSEPR applied to each printed species to get its shape, demonstrated by 'both the species are not isostructural' set against $\mathrm{XeF_4}, \mathrm{XeO_4}$, whose shapes part only once the two lone pairs on $\mathrm{XeF_4}$ are counted.
- **Note:** The pattern returned plain_mcq, but each option is a pair of species accepted or rejected whole, so the shape on the page is a pair option. The retired E1 value pair_options has no direct successor among the six current qtype values, and two_part is the nearest, since the answer must hold of both members together. Flagging the mapping rather than flattening it to mcq.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q14

Chemistry · organic · Hydrocarbons

In the reaction with $\mathrm{HCl}$, an alkene reacts in accordance with the Markovnikov’s rule, to give a product 1-chloro-1-methylcyclohexane. The possible reaction alkene is:

- (1) methylenecyclohexane `C=C1CCCCC1`
- (2) 1-methylcyclohexene `CC1=CCCCC1`
- **(3)** methylenecyclohexane `C=C1CCCCC1`; 1-methylcyclohexene `CC1=CCCCC1` — and
- (4) 3-methylcyclohexene `CC1C=CCCC1`

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: methylenecyclohexane →(H⁺) 1-methylcyclohexyl cation →(Cl⁻) 1-chloro-1-methylcyclohexane](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q014_sol_1_b9958495_28102026.png?v=b9958495f59a)

*methylenecyclohexane →[H⁺] 1-methylcyclohexyl cation →[Cl⁻] 1-chloro-1-methylcyclohexane*


**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Markovnikov addition: $\mathrm{H^+}$ adds to the alkene carbon that gives the more stable (tertiary) carbocation.
2. Methylenecyclohexane ($\mathrm{C_6H_{10}{=}CH_2}$): $\mathrm{H^+}$ adds to $\mathrm{=CH_2}$, giving the tertiary cation at ring $C_1$ bearing $\mathrm{CH_3}$.
3. 1-Methylcyclohexene: $\mathrm{H^+}$ adds to $C_2$, giving the same tertiary cation at $C_1$ bearing $\mathrm{CH_3}$.
4. $\mathrm{Cl^-}$ attacks this cation to give 1-chloro-1-methylcyclohexane from both alkenes.
5. 3-Methylcyclohexene (option $4$) gives only secondary cations on $C_1$/$C_2$, not the 1,1-disubstituted product, so the answer is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Chemical Properties of Alkenes · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 5)

![printed question 14](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q014_0d97e5d5_28102026.png?v=0d97e5d57200)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 14](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q014_c7664c06_28102026.png?v=c7664c062ce6)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Reaction outcome** (`reaction`) — what a reaction gives, or the reagent it needs
- **Concept:** Markovnikov addition puts the chlorine on the more substituted carbon, so the alkene must carry its double bond at the ring carbon that already bears the methyl group
- **Trap:** **No trap** (`no_trap`) — nothing pulls; the other options are only filler  
  Options 1, 2 and 4 arrived as empty strings and option 3 carries only the word 'and', so the four structure drawings are not in this packet and no distractor can be read, let alone a route to one.
- **Why:** This question requires Markovnikov's rule run backwards from a printed product to the alkene that gives it, demonstrated by 'to give a product 1-chloro-1-methylcyclohexane. The possible reaction alkene is'.
- **Note:** Source incomplete: the option set is four structure images that did not survive extraction, and option 3, the printed key, retains only the connecting word 'and', which suggests two structures were offered together. The stem and key are intact and are tagged as printed. A reviewer holding the paper should check whether both the ring alkene and the exocyclic alkene appear in option 3, since Markovnikov addition of $\mathrm{HCl}$ to either gives the same tertiary chloride; that is the mechanism to confirm, not a finding from this record.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q15

Chemistry · physical · Redox Reactions

Assuming complete ionization, same moles of which of the following compounds will require the least amount of acidified $\mathrm{KMnO_4}$ for complete oxidation?

- (1) $\mathrm{FeC_2O_4}$
- (2) $\mathrm{Fe(NO_2)_2}$
- **(3)** $\mathrm{FeSO_4}$
- (4) $\mathrm{FeSO_3}$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mathrm{MnO_4^{-}} \longrightarrow \mathrm{Mn^{2+}}$ ; Change in oxidation no. $= 5$
2. In option,
3. i. $\mathrm{Fe^{2+}} \longrightarrow \mathrm{Fe^{3+}}$
4. Change in oxidation no. $= 1$
5. ii. $\mathrm{Fe^{2+}} \longrightarrow \mathrm{Fe^{3+}}$
6. Change in oxidation no. $= 1$
7. $\mathrm{SO_3^{2-}} \longrightarrow \mathrm{SO_4^{2-}}$
8. Change in oxidation no. $= 2$
9. $= 1 + 2 = 3$
10. iii. $\mathrm{Fe^{2+}} \longrightarrow \mathrm{Fe^{3+}}$
11. Change in oxidation no. $= 1$
12. $\mathrm{C_2O_4^{2-}} \longrightarrow 2\mathrm{CO_2}$
13. Change in oxidation no. $= 2$
14. $= 1 + 2 = 3$
15. iv. $\mathrm{Fe^{2+}} \longrightarrow \mathrm{Fe^{3+}}$
16. Change in oxidation no. $= 1$
17. $2\mathrm{NO_2^{-}} \longrightarrow 2\mathrm{NO_3^{-}}$
18. Change in oxidation no. $= 4$
19. $= 1 + 4 = 5$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Acidified $\mathrm{KMnO_4}$ needed is proportional to the electrons lost per mole of reductant.
2. $\mathrm{FeC_2O_4}$: $\mathrm{Fe^{2+} \to Fe^{3+}}$ ($1e^-$) and $\mathrm{C_2O_4^{2-} \to 2CO_2}$ ($2e^-$), total $3e^-$.
3. $\mathrm{Fe(NO_2)_2}$: $\mathrm{Fe^{2+} \to Fe^{3+}}$ ($1e^-$) and $2\,\mathrm{NO_2^- \to 2NO_3^-}$ ($2 \times 2 = 4e^-$), total $5e^-$.
4. $\mathrm{FeSO_4}$: only $\mathrm{Fe^{2+} \to Fe^{3+}}$ ($1e^-$); $\mathrm{SO_4^{2-}}$ is not oxidised, total $1e^-$.
5. $\mathrm{FeSO_3}$: $\mathrm{Fe^{2+} \to Fe^{3+}}$ ($1e^-$) and $\mathrm{SO_3^{2-} \to SO_4^{2-}}$ ($2e^-$), total $3e^-$.
6. Fewest electrons is $\mathrm{FeSO_4}$ ($1e^-$), so it needs the least $\mathrm{KMnO_4}$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** redox reactions as the basis for titrations using potassium permanganate – practice · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 6)

![printed question 15](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q015_0e808dc9_28102026.png?v=0e808dc9028b)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 15](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q015_311eb3ab_28102026.png?v=311eb3abd6ee)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Permanganate consumed per mole is set by the total electrons the salt can give up: the $\mathrm{Fe^{2+}}$ plus any oxidisable anion
- **Trap:** **look alike term** (`look_alike_term`) — two terms RESEMBLE each other and the resemblance is the pull: maturation / meristematic, lyase / ligase, GPP / NPP — option 4  
  $\mathrm{FeSO_3}$ differs from the key $\mathrm{FeSO_4}$ by one oxygen, so 'sulphate is a spectator in a permanganate titration' gets carried across to sulphite; sulphite is itself oxidised to sulphate, which makes $\mathrm{FeSO_3}$ 3 electrons per mole against 1 for $\mathrm{FeSO_4}$.
- **Why:** This question requires the electron count per mole worked out for each salt, demonstrated by 'same moles of which of the following compounds will require the least amount of acidified $\mathrm{KMnO_4}$' printed against three oxidisable anions and one that is not.
- **Note:** look_alike_term chosen over nearby_fact by the swap test named in 5.1: replace $\mathrm{FeSO_3}$ with an anion that does not resemble sulphate and nothing pulls, so the one-oxygen likeness is carrying the student, not table position. asks is calculate rather than compare because the decision rests on a number produced per option, 1, 3, 5 and 3 electrons, even though the answer form is a formula.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q16

Chemistry · organic · Alcohols, Phenols and Ethers

Reaction of phenol with chloroform in presence of dilute sodium hydroxide finally introduces which one of the following functional group?

- (1) $-\mathrm{CHCl_2}$
- **(2)** $-\mathrm{CHO}$
- (3) $-\mathrm{CH_2Cl}$
- (4) $-\mathrm{COOH}$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Reimer Tieman reaction

![printed figure: phenol + $\ce{CHCl3}$ + $\ce{NaOH}$ → 2-hydroxybenzaldehyde > 4-hydroxybenzaldehyde](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q016_sol_1_67aef367_28102026.png?v=67aef3677b9d)

*phenol + $\ce{CHCl3}$ + $\ce{NaOH}$ → 2-hydroxybenzaldehyde > 4-hydroxybenzaldehyde*


**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Phenol with $\mathrm{CHCl_3}$ and dilute $\mathrm{NaOH}$ is the Reimer-Tiemann reaction; dichlorocarbene ($:\mathrm{CCl_2}$) attacks the ortho position of phenoxide, giving a $-\mathrm{CHCl_2}$ intermediate.
2. In alkali the $-\mathrm{CHCl_2}$ group is hydrolysed to $-\mathrm{CHO}$, so the final product is salicylaldehyde and the group introduced is $-\mathrm{CHO}$, option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Reactions of Phenols · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 7)

![printed question 16](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q016_ec354be2_28102026.png?v=ec354be28dfa)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Reaction outcome** (`reaction`) — what a reaction gives, or the reagent it needs
- **Concept:** Reimer-Tiemann: dichlorocarbene installs $-\mathrm{CHCl_2}$ ortho to the phenolic $\mathrm{OH}$, and the alkali then hydrolyses it to $-\mathrm{CHO}$
- **Trap:** **Answers another question** (`answers_other_question`) — true, and about this topic, but it answers something else — option 1  
  $-\mathrm{CHCl_2}$ is the group the dichlorocarbene really does install, so running the mechanism to the carbene attack and stopping there reports it; the stem asks what is present 'finally', and the alkali already in the flask hydrolyses $-\mathrm{CHCl_2}$ to $-\mathrm{CHO}$.
- **Why:** This question requires the product of a named reagent pair acting on phenol, demonstrated by 'chloroform in presence of dilute sodium hydroxide finally introduces which one of the following functional group'.

## Q17

Chemistry · physical · The Solid State

The vacant space in bcc lattice unit cell is:

- (1) $23\%$
- **(2)** $32\%$
- (3) $26\%$
- (4) $48\%$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Packing efficiency in bcc lattice $= 68\%$
2. $\therefore$ Vacant space in bcc lattice $= 100 - 68 = 32\%$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. A bcc unit cell holds $Z = 2$ atoms, which touch along the body diagonal: $\sqrt{3}a = 4r$, so $r = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{4}a$.
2. Packing fraction $= \frac{2 \times \frac{4}{3}\pi r^3}{a^3} = \frac{\sqrt{3}\pi}{8} \approx 0.68$, i.e. $68\%$.
3. Vacant space $= 100\% - 68\% = 32\%$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Close Packing, Tetrahedral and Octahedral Voids and Formula of Compounds · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 7)

![printed question 17](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q017_8569d87d_28102026.png?v=8569d87dd532)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 17](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q017_a01e7df0_28102026.png?v=a01e7df03b9b)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Stated fact** (`fact_line`) — a fact NCERT states: what, who or where
- **Concept:** A body-centred cubic cell packs 68% of its volume, so the empty space is the complement, 32%
- **Trap:** **Neighbouring fact** (`nearby_fact`) — a true fact about a neighbouring thing is offered instead — option 3  
  26% is the vacant space on the ccp and hcp line of the same packing-efficiency table, one row from bcc, so reaching into that table and landing on the 74% entry returns $100-74=26$ in place of $100-68=32$.
- **Why:** This question requires NCERT's stored packing efficiency for the body-centred cell, demonstrated by 'the vacant space in bcc lattice unit cell is' with no edge length, radius or sphere count supplied to compute from.
- **Note:** asks is fact_line rather than calculate because the stem supplies no numbers at all; the single step past the stored value is its complement, and the printed answer line, 'Packing efficiency in bcc lattice = 68%', is that stored value.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q18

Chemistry · inorganic · The p–Block Elements

Which of the statements given below is incorrect?

- (1) $\mathrm{ONF}$ is isoelectronic with $\mathrm{O_2N^-}$
- **(2)** $\mathrm{OF_2}$ is an oxide of fluorine
- (3) $\mathrm{Cl_2O_7}$ is an anhydride of perchloric acid
- (4) $\mathrm{O_3}$ molecule is bent

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. No. of electron in $\mathrm{ONF} = 24$
2. No. of electron in $\mathrm{NO_2^-} = 24$
3. Both are isoelectronic.
4. $\mathrm{OF_2}$ is a fluoride of oxygen not oxide of fluorine because EN of fluorine is more than oxygen.
5. $\mathrm{OF_2} =$ Oxygen difluoride
6. $\mathrm{Cl_2O_7}$ is an anhydride of perchloric acid.
7. $\mathrm{2HClO_4} \xrightarrow{\Delta,\,-\mathrm{H_2O}} \mathrm{Cl_2O_7}$
8. $\mathrm{O_3}$ molecules is bent shape.

![printed figure: ozone ($\ce{O3}$), bent: central O carrying a lone pair, a coordinate bond (arrow) to the left O and a double bond to the right O](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q018_sol_1_6d8f89ab_28102026.png?v=6d8f89ab9889)

*ozone ($\ce{O3}$), bent: central O carrying a lone pair, a coordinate bond (arrow) to the left O and a double bond to the right O*


**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Electrons in $\mathrm{ONF}$: $8 + 7 + 9 = 24$; in $\mathrm{O_2N^-}$: $2(8) + 7 + 1 = 24$, so statement 1 is correct.
2. Fluorine is more electronegative than oxygen, so in $\mathrm{OF_2}$ oxygen is the positive partner and the compound is oxygen difluoride, a fluoride of oxygen, not an oxide of fluorine; statement 2 is incorrect.
3. $\mathrm{Cl_2O_7 + H_2O \rightarrow 2HClO_4}$, so $\mathrm{Cl_2O_7}$ is the anhydride of perchloric acid; statement 3 is correct.
4. $\mathrm{O_3}$ has a lone pair on the central O and a bond angle of about $117^\circ$, so it is bent; statement 4 is correct.
5. The incorrect statement is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** General Properties & Trends of Group 17 (Halogens) · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 7)

![printed question 18](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q018_7498d5ad_28102026.png?v=7498d5ad375e)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 18](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q018_2af18da4_28102026.png?v=2af18da46694)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Statements** (`statements`) — lettered statements, the answer is which combination is right
- **Asks:** **Stated fact** (`fact_line`) — a fact NCERT states: what, who or where
- **Concept:** In a binary compound the more electronegative element takes the -ide name, so $\mathrm{OF_2}$ is oxygen difluoride, a fluoride of oxygen and not an oxide of fluorine
- **Trap:** **Arithmetic slip** (`arithmetic_slip`) — the number or the unit is wrong, or a step was stopped short — option 1  
  Totalling the valence electrons of $\mathrm{O_2N^-}$ while leaving out the electron the negative charge brings gives 17 against 18 for $\mathrm{ONF}$, so the isoelectronic claim comes out false and gets marked as the incorrect statement.
- **Why:** This question requires each printed claim judged against the line NCERT states, demonstrated by '$\mathrm{OF_2}$ is an oxide of fluorine' being the one that inverts the naming rule, fluorine being the more electronegative of the two.
- **Note:** qtype is statements because the stem itself says 'which of the statements given below is incorrect', which is the test in rule 1, and the pattern's statement_options agrees. The task is negated, so the three wrong options are all true claims; the trap is placed on the one whose check has a nameable failing step rather than spread across all three, since a polarity flip alone selects no single option.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q19

Chemistry · inorganic · Coordination Compounds – Practice

The name of complex ion, $\mathrm{[Fe(CN)_6]^{3-}}$ is :

- (1) Tricyanoferrate (III) ion
- **(2)** Hexacyanidoferrate (III) ion
- (3) Hexacyanoiron (III) ion
- (4) Hexacyanitoferrate (III) ion

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mathrm{[Fe(CN)_6]^{3-}}$
2. Hexacyanidoferrate (III) ion

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. There are six $\mathrm{CN^-}$ ligands, named $\text{cyanido}$ under IUPAC rules, so the ligand part is $\text{hexacyanido}$.
2. Oxidation state of Fe: $x + 6(-1) = -3 \Rightarrow x = +3$.
3. The complex ion is an anion, so the metal takes the $\text{-ate}$ ending with its Latin root: $\text{ferrate}$.
4. Name: $\text{hexacyanidoferrate(III) ion}$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** IUPAC Nomenclature and Formulae of Coordination Compounds · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 8)

![printed question 19](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q019_f7a6330b_28102026.png?v=f7a6330b10a3)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 19](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q019_74b7a6d3_28102026.png?v=74b7a6d38e95)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** Naming a complex anion: ligands first with the -ido ending, then the metal's Latin stem with -ate, with the oxidation state in brackets
- **Trap:** **look alike term** (`look_alike_term`) — two terms RESEMBLE each other and the resemblance is the pull: maturation / meristematic, lyase / ligase, GPP / NPP — option 4  
  The -ito ending is a real ligand suffix - nitrito, sulfito - so a student who knows the modern ligand name ends in that kind of cluster but not which letter reaches for 'cyanito'. The ligand $\mathrm{CN^-}$ is named cyanido, so only option 2 spells it as the convention does.
- **Why:** This question requires the IUPAC rules for naming a complex anion run over a printed formula, demonstrated by the stem supplying only '$\mathrm{[Fe(CN)_6]^{3-}}$' while the options differ one rule-element at a time - the count prefix, the ligand suffix, and whether the metal is written 'iron' or 'ferrate'.
- **Note:** Ruled out old_ncert_answer: the older textbook form 'hexacyanoferrate(III)' is not printed among the options, so there is no altered line with the book's own answer sitting as a distractor. Applied the §5.1 swap test on option 4 - replace 'cyanito' with an unlike word and the pull dies, so likeness is doing the work, not position.

## Q20

Chemistry · physical · Basic Concepts of Chemistry

If Avogadro number $\mathrm{N_A}$ , is changed from $6.022 \times 10^{23}\,\mathrm{mol^{-1}}$ to $6.022 \times 10^{20}\,\mathrm{mol^{-1}}$, this would change:

- (1) The ratio of chemical species to each other in a balanced equation.
- (2) The ratio of elements to each other in a compound.
- (3) The definition of mass in units of grams
- **(4)** The mass of one mole of carbon

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\because$ Mass of $1$ mol ($6.022 \times 10^{23}$ atoms) of carbon $= 12\,\mathrm{g}$
2. If Avogadro Number ($\mathrm{N_A}$) is changed then mass of $1$ mol ($6.022 \times 10^{20}$ atom) of carbon
3. $= \frac{12 \times 6.022 \times 10^{20}}{6.022 \times 10^{23}} = 12 \times 10^{-3}\,\mathrm{g}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Ratios of species in a balanced equation and of elements in a compound are ratios of numbers of particles, so they do not depend on $N_A$; options $1$ and $2$ are unchanged.
2. The gram is a unit of mass defined independently of $N_A$, so option $3$ is unchanged.
3. Mass of one mole of carbon $= N_A \times m_{\mathrm{C\,atom}} = 6.022 \times 10^{20} \times 1.993 \times 10^{-23}\,\mathrm{g} \approx 0.012\,\mathrm{g}$, which differs from $12\,\mathrm{g}$.
4. Only the mass of one mole of carbon changes, which is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** Mole Concept · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 8)

![printed question 20](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q020_1d3519be_28102026.png?v=1d3519be2ab5)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 20](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q020_c4d99e2e_28102026.png?v=c4d99e2e1bc7)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** The mole is a counting unit: Avogadro's number fixes how many particles a mole holds and so fixes molar mass, while a ratio of amounts is a pure number and does not move with it
- **Trap:** **Neighbouring fact** (`nearby_fact`) — a true fact about a neighbouring thing is offered instead — option 3  
  Avogadro's number is introduced in the same passage as the gram-scale definition - it is the count of atoms in 12 g of carbon-12 - so a student holding that pairing concludes that moving the count must move the gram. The gram is fixed independently of any count; what moves is the mass of one mole of carbon, from 12 g to 0.012 g.
- **Why:** This question requires the definition of the mole applied to a hypothetical change in a constant, demonstrated by the stem's '$\mathrm{N_A}$ ... changed from $6.022\times10^{23}$ to $6.022\times10^{20}$' and four options naming different things the change might touch.
- **Note:** E1's pattern read the four options as full statements, but they are noun phrases completing the stem 'this would change:', not claims to be graded, so the printed shape is a plain MCQ. Applied the §5.1 swap test on option 3: 'the definition of mass in units of grams' is not a name resembling 'the mass of one mole of carbon' - it is the line printed beside it in the definition of the mole, so position carries the student and the label is nearby_fact.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q21

Chemistry · organic · General Organic Chemistry

Which of the following statements is not correct for a nucleophile?

- (1) Nucleophiles attack low $\mathrm{e^{-}}$ density sites
- (2) Nucleophiles are not electron seeking
- **(3)** Nucleophile is a Lewis acid
- (4) Ammonia is a nucleophile

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Reason: Nucleophiles are electron rich species so act as Lewis base.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. A nucleophile ($\mathrm{Nu^{:}}$) is nucleus-seeking: it donates an electron pair and attacks electron-deficient (low $\mathrm{e^{-}}$ density) sites, so statements $1$ and $2$ are correct.
2. $\mathrm{NH_3}$ has a lone pair on N, so ammonia is a nucleophile; statement $4$ is correct.
3. An electron-pair donor is a Lewis base, not a Lewis acid, so statement $3$ is not correct, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Electrophile and Nucleophile · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 8)

![printed question 21](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q021_3134b214_28102026.png?v=3134b214ed75)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 21](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q021_7f88e9e4_28102026.png?v=7f88e9e438f6)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Statements** (`statements`) — lettered statements, the answer is which combination is right
- **Asks:** **Stated fact** (`fact_line`) — a fact NCERT states: what, who or where
- **Concept:** A nucleophile is an electron-rich species that donates an electron pair, which makes it a Lewis base and never a Lewis acid
- **Trap:** **Answers another question** (`answers_other_question`) — true, and about this topic, but it answers something else — option 2  
  'Nucleophiles are not electron seeking' is the only option whose own wording is negative, so a student tracking one negation reads it as the statement that clashes with the stem. Nucleophiles really are not electron-seeking - that describes electrophiles - so this statement is true, and ticking it answers 'which statement is correct' instead of the question asked.
- **Why:** This question requires the stated definition of a nucleophile as an electron-rich electron-pair donor, demonstrated by 'Nucleophile is a Lewis acid' being the one option that contradicts it while the other three restate it.
- **Note:** E1's statement_options maps onto qtype statements here because the stem itself says 'Which of the following statements is not correct', which is the test in the brief's rule 1. The trap turns on the negated task rather than on chemistry: option 2 is a true statement.

## Q22

Chemistry · physical · States of Matter

A gas such as carbon monoxide would be most likely to obey the ideal gas law at:

- (1) High temperatures and high pressures
- (2) Low temperatures and low pressures
- **(3)** High temperatures and low pressures
- (4) Low temperatures and high pressures

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Real gases show ideal gas behaviour at high temperatures and low pressures.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Ideal behaviour needs negligible molecular volume and negligible intermolecular attraction, i.e. compressibility factor $Z = \frac{PV}{nRT} \to 1$.
2. At low pressure the gas volume is large, so the volume of the molecules themselves is negligible.
3. At high temperature the kinetic energy is large, so intermolecular attractions are negligible; hence high $T$ and low $P$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Real Gases and van der Waals Equation · **Difficulty:** easy (1 step)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 8)

![printed question 22](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q022_c3f4ef47_28102026.png?v=c3f4ef472556)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 22](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q022_741d355b_28102026.png?v=741d355b94f3)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Stated fact** (`fact_line`) — a fact NCERT states: what, who or where
- **Concept:** Real gases approach ideal behaviour where intermolecular attraction and molecular volume stop mattering - high temperature and low pressure
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 4  
  The sentence carried out of this chapter is that real gases deviate most at low temperature and high pressure, and option 4 prints exactly that pair. The stem asks for the corner where the gas obeys the law, which is the opposite end of the same sentence.
- **Why:** This question requires the stated condition under which a real gas behaves ideally, demonstrated by the stem offering no property of carbon monoxide to work from - 'a gas such as carbon monoxide' - while the options are the four corners of high or low temperature crossed with high or low pressure.
- **Note:** E1's pattern read the options as full statements; they are temperature-pressure condition pairs, so the printed shape is a plain MCQ. Options 1 and 2 each keep one of the two conditions, so half_right would describe two options at once rather than naming the single catch; option 4 prints the remembered deviation condition whole, which is why reversed is the question's trap.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q23

Chemistry · inorganic · Coordination Compounds – Practice

The hybridization involved in complex $\mathrm{[Ni(CN)_4]^{2-}}$ is: (Atomic number of Ni = $28$)

- (1) $\mathrm{d^2sp^2}$
- (2) $\mathrm{d^2sp^3}$
- **(3)** $\mathrm{dsp^2}$
- (4) $\mathrm{sp^3}$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mathrm{[Ni(CN)_4]^{2-}}$
2. Oxidation state of $\mathrm{Ni}$ is $+2$
3. $x - 4 = 2$
4. $x = +2$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Oxidation state of Ni: $x + 4(-1) = -2 \Rightarrow x = +2$.
2. $\mathrm{Ni}$ ($Z=28$): $[\mathrm{Ar}]\,3d^8 4s^2$, so $\mathrm{Ni^{2+}}$: $[\mathrm{Ar}]\,3d^8$.
3. $\mathrm{CN^-}$ is a strong-field ligand, so the eight $3d$ electrons pair up in four $3d$ orbitals, leaving one $3d$ orbital empty.
4. Four ligands use one $3d$, one $4s$ and two $4p$ orbitals: $\mathrm{dsp^2}$ hybridization, square planar, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Valence Bond Theory, Its Limitations and Magnetic Properties of Coordination Compounds · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 8)

![printed question 23](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q023_c71fc951_28102026.png?v=c71fc951f849)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 23](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q023_46f5c9db_28102026.png?v=46f5c9db8463)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** Valence bond theory for four-coordinate Ni(II): a strong-field ligand pairs the $d^8$ electrons, frees a $3d$ orbital and gives square planar $dsp^2$ instead of tetrahedral $sp^3$
- **Trap:** **Condition dropped** (`condition_missed`) — drop the condition the stem states and the option becomes true — option 4  
  Four ligands around Ni(II) is the picture of $\mathrm{[NiCl_4]^{2-}}$, which is tetrahedral $sp^3$, so a student who reads the coordination number and stops carries that result across. $\mathrm{CN^-}$ is strong-field: it pairs the $d^8$ electrons, frees a $3d$ orbital, and the hybridisation is $dsp^2$.
- **Why:** This question requires valence bond theory applied to a named complex, demonstrated by the stem supplying 'Atomic number of Ni = $28$' so that the $d^8$ count and the four $\mathrm{CN^-}$ ligands together decide the hybridisation.
- **Note:** condition_missed rather than new_trap, per the §5.1 reading that it covers a remembered result used past the point its own condition holds: 'four-coordinate Ni(II) is tetrahedral $sp^3$' holds for weak-field ligands, and the printed ligand $\mathrm{CN^-}$ is where it breaks.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q24

Chemistry · physical · Thermodynamics

The heat of combustion of carbon to $\mathrm{CO_2}$ is $-393.5\,\mathrm{kJ/mol}$. The heat released upon formation of $35.2\,\mathrm{g}$ of $\mathrm{CO_2}$ from carbon and oxygen gas is:

- (1) $-630\,\mathrm{kJ}$
- (2) $-3.15\,\mathrm{kJ}$
- **(3)** $-315\,\mathrm{kJ}$
- (4) $+315\,\mathrm{kJ}$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Formation of $\mathrm{CO_2}$ from carbon and dioxygen gas can be represented as
2. $\mathrm{C(s)} + \mathrm{O_{2(g)}} \rightarrow \mathrm{CO_{2(g)}}\,;\ \Delta_f H = -393.5\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$
3. ($1\,\mathrm{mole} = 44\,\mathrm{g}$)
4. Heat released on formation of $44\,\mathrm{g}$ $\mathrm{CO_2}$
5. $= -393.5\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}$
6. $= \frac{-393.5\,\mathrm{kJ\,mol^{-1}}}{44\,\mathrm{g}} \times 35.2\,\mathrm{g}$
7. $= -315\,\mathrm{kJ}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Molar mass of $\mathrm{CO_2}$ is $12 + 2\times16 = 44\,\mathrm{g/mol}$.
2. $n = \frac{35.2\,\mathrm{g}}{44\,\mathrm{g/mol}} = 0.8\,\mathrm{mol}$.
3. $\Delta H = 0.8 \times (-393.5\,\mathrm{kJ/mol}) = -314.8\,\mathrm{kJ} \approx -315\,\mathrm{kJ}$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Thermochemical Equations · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 9)

![printed question 24](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q024_e9f94b0a_28102026.png?v=e9f94b0afeb5)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 24](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q024_267dc9d1_28102026.png?v=267dc9d1e245)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Scaling a molar enthalpy of combustion by the amount actually formed, and keeping the negative sign an exothermic change carries
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 4  
  The stem asks for the heat RELEASED, and heat given out is naturally written as a positive amount, so a student with the magnitude $315$ kJ right signs it $+$. Released heat is an exothermic enthalpy change, and the stem's own datum is printed as $-393.5$ kJ/mol.
- **Why:** This question requires a per-mole enthalpy scaled by the amount formed, demonstrated by the stem giving '$-393.5$ kJ/mol' and asking for the heat on forming '$35.2$ g of $\mathrm{CO_2}$'.
- **Note:** The exact value is $-314.8$ kJ and the key prints $-315$ kJ; the rounding is the paper's own and does not bear on the tagging.

## Q25

Chemistry · physical · Basic Concepts of Chemistry

$20.0\,\mathrm{g}$ of a magnesium carbonate sample decomposes on heating to give carbon dioxide and $8.0\,\mathrm{g}$ magnesium oxide. What will be the percentage purity of magnesium carbonate in the sample? (Atomic weight: $\mathrm{Mg} = 24$)

- (1) 60
- **(2)** 84
- (3) 75
- (4) 96

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mathrm{MgCO_3(s)} \longrightarrow \mathrm{MgO(s)} + \mathrm{CO_2(g)}$
2. Moles of $\mathrm{MgCO_3} = \frac{20}{84} = 0.238\,\mathrm{mol}$
3. From above equation.
4. $1$ mole $\mathrm{MgCO_3}$ gives $1$ mole $\mathrm{MgO}$
5. $\therefore$ $0.238$ mole $\mathrm{MgCO_3}$ will give $0.238$ mole $\mathrm{MgO}$
6. $= 0.238 \times 40\,\mathrm{g} = 9.523\,\mathrm{g}\ \mathrm{MgO}$
7. Practical yield of $\mathrm{MgO} = 8\,\mathrm{g}\ \mathrm{MgO}$
8. $\therefore \%\ \text{Purity} = \frac{8}{9.523} \times 100 = 84\%$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. $\mathrm{MgCO_3 \rightarrow MgO + CO_2}$, so 1 mol $\mathrm{MgCO_3}$ gives 1 mol $\mathrm{MgO}$.
2. $M(\mathrm{MgO}) = 24 + 16 = 40\,\mathrm{g\,mol^{-1}}$ and $M(\mathrm{MgCO_3}) = 24 + 12 + 3(16) = 84\,\mathrm{g\,mol^{-1}}$.
3. $n(\mathrm{MgO}) = \frac{8.0}{40} = 0.2\,\mathrm{mol}$.
4. $m(\mathrm{MgCO_3}) = 0.2 \times 84 = 16.8\,\mathrm{g}$.
5. Purity $= \frac{16.8}{20.0} \times 100 = 84\%$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Stoichiometry · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 9)

![printed question 25](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q025_89a8f4e1_28102026.png?v=89a8f4e1536d)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 25](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q025_1bb29c7b_28102026.png?v=1bb29c7bd7e5)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Percentage purity by stoichiometry: convert the weighed product back to the reactant through the balanced equation, then divide by the sample mass
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 1  
  Reading the 8.0 g of MgO left in the dish as the part of the sample that was never carbonate makes the 12 g that vanished look like the carbonate, and $12/20$ prints as 60. The oxide came from the carbonate: $8.0/40 = 0.2$ mol of MgO means $0.2 \times 84 = 16.8$ g of MgCO$_3$, so 84.
- **Why:** This question requires a number computed from the stem's masses through the balanced decomposition, demonstrated by 'What will be the percentage purity of magnesium carbonate in the sample?' with the atomic weight of Mg supplied and all four options bare numbers.

## Q26

Chemistry · physical · Basic Concepts of Chemistry

What is the mole fraction of the solute in a $1.00\,\mathrm{m}$ aqueous solution?

- (1) 0.0354
- **(2)** 0.0177
- (3) 0.177
- (4) 1.770

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $1.0\,\mathrm{m}$ solution means $1$ mole solute is present in $1000\,\mathrm{g}$ water.
2. $n_{\mathrm{H_2O}} = 55.5\,\mathrm{mol}\ \mathrm{H_2O}$
3. $X_{\mathrm{Solute}} = \frac{n_{\mathrm{Solute}}}{n_{\mathrm{Solute}} + n_{\mathrm{H_2O}}} = \frac{1}{1 + 55.5} = 0.0177$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. A $1.00\,\mathrm{m}$ solution has $1\,\mathrm{mol}$ of solute in $1000\,\mathrm{g}$ of water.
2. $n_{\mathrm{H_2O}} = \frac{1000}{18} = 55.51\,\mathrm{mol}$.
3. $x_{\text{solute}} = \frac{1}{1 + 55.51} = \frac{1}{56.51} = 0.0177$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Mole Fraction · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 9)

![printed question 26](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q026_09234a55_28102026.png?v=09234a55c109)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 26](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q026_215cef0d_28102026.png?v=215cef0d44c0)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Mole fraction from molality: 1 mol of solute sits in 1000 g of water, which is 55.5 mol, and the solute's own mole belongs in the total
- **Trap:** **Arithmetic slip** (`arithmetic_slip`) — the number or the unit is wrong, or a step was stopped short — option 3  
  The setup $1/(1 + 55.5)$ is right and the reciprocal of 56.5 is taken one decimal place out, printing 0.177 — exactly ten times the answer. One solute mole among 55.5 water moles is one part in about 56, so the figure has to begin 0.01.
- **Why:** This question requires a number computed from the stem's concentration, demonstrated by 'the mole fraction of the solute in a $1.00\,\mathrm{m}$ aqueous solution' with four numerical options.
- **Note:** The one conceptual slip this calculation invites — leaving the solute's own mole out of the total, $1/55.5 = 0.0180$ — is not printed and rounds onto the key, so the three wrong options are the key scaled by 2, 10 and 100.
