## Q160

Physics · Modern Physics

Light of wavelength $500\,\mathrm{nm}$ is incident on a metal with work function $2.258\,\mathrm{eV}$. The de Broglie wavelength of the emitted electron is:

- (1) $\leq 2.8 \times 10^{-12}\,\mathrm{m}$
- (2) $< 2.8 \times 10^{-10}\,\mathrm{m}$
- (3) $< 2.8 \times 10^{-9}\,\mathrm{m}$
- **(4)** $\geq 2.8 \times 10^{-9}\,\mathrm{m}$

**Answer** — booklet: (4) · independent solve: (4) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mathrm{KE_{max}} = \frac{hc}{\lambda} - \Psi$
2. $\mathrm{KE_{max}} = \frac{1240}{500} - 2.82$
3. $\mathrm{KE_{max}} = 2.48 - 2.28 = 0.2\,\mathrm{eV}$
4. $\lambda_{\mathrm{min}} = \frac{h}{\sqrt{2m(\mathrm{KE})_{\mathrm{max}}}} = \frac{\frac{20}{3} \times 10^{-34}}{\sqrt{2 \times 9 \times 10^{-31} \times 0.2 \times 1.6 \times 10^{-19}}}$
5. $\lambda_{\mathrm{min}} = \frac{25}{9} \times 10^{-9} = 2.80 \times 10^{-9}\,\mathrm{nm}$
6. So $\lambda \geq 2.8 \times 10^{-9}\,\mathrm{m}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Photon energy: $E = \frac{hc}{\lambda} = \frac{1240\,\mathrm{eV\,nm}}{500\,\mathrm{nm}} = 2.48\,\mathrm{eV}$.
2. Maximum kinetic energy: $K_{max} = E - \phi = 2.48 - 2.258 = 0.222\,\mathrm{eV}$.
3. Convert: $K_{max} = 0.222 \times 1.6\times10^{-19}\,\mathrm{J} = 3.55\times10^{-20}\,\mathrm{J}$.
4. Momentum: $p_{max} = \sqrt{2mK_{max}} = \sqrt{2 \times 9.1\times10^{-31} \times 3.55\times10^{-20}} \approx 2.54\times10^{-25}\,\mathrm{kg\,m/s}$.
5. Minimum wavelength: $\lambda_{min} = \frac{h}{p_{max}} = \frac{6.63\times10^{-34}}{2.54\times10^{-25}} \approx 2.6\times10^{-9}\,\mathrm{m}$, i.e. of order $2.8\times10^{-9}\,\mathrm{m}$.
6. Emitted electrons have $K \leq K_{max}$, so $\lambda \geq \lambda_{min}$; the 'greater than or equal' form is option $4$.

**Subtopic:** de Broglie Wavelength of Matter Waves, Photoelectric Effect · **Difficulty:** hard (4 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 46)

![printed question 160](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q160_f602db60_28102026.png?v=f602db60cadd)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 160](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q160_ce5bce6a_28102026.png?v=ce5bce6a9a25)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Photoelectrons leave with kinetic energies anywhere from zero up to $h\nu-\phi$, so their de Broglie wavelength has a floor and no ceiling
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 3  
  The wavelength is computed from the maximum kinetic energy and then read as a ceiling, because maximum energy feels like a maximum answer. The fastest electron carries the SHORTEST wavelength, so the computed value is the floor and the answer set is $\lambda \geq 2.8 \times 10^{-9}\,\mathrm{m}$.
- **Why:** This question requires the maximum photoelectron energy to be computed and then the side of that wavelength on which the answer set lies to be fixed, demonstrated by two options carrying the same magnitude $2.8 \times 10^{-9}\,\mathrm{m}$ and differing only in $<$ against $\geq$.
- **Note:** Arithmetic check on the printed numbers: $1240/500 = 2.48\,\mathrm{eV}$, minus $2.258\,\mathrm{eV}$ leaves $0.222\,\mathrm{eV}$, and $1.227/\sqrt{0.222} = 2.6\,\mathrm{nm}$, not $2.8\,\mathrm{nm}$; the printed $2.8\,\mathrm{nm}$ follows from a work function of about $2.28\,\mathrm{eV}$, so the stem may have gained a digit. Tagged as printed, and the inequality direction that decides the key is unaffected either way.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q161

Physics · Waves

$4.0\,\mathrm{g}$ of a gas occupies $22.4$ liters at NTP. The specific heat capacity of the gas at constant volume is $5.0\,\mathrm{JK^{-1}\,mol^{-1}}$. If the speed of sound in this gas at NTP is $952\,\mathrm{ms^{-1}}$, then the heat capacity at constant pressure is (Take gas constant $\mathrm{R} = 8.3\,\mathrm{JK^{-1}\,mol^{-1}}$)

- (1) $8.5\,\mathrm{JK^{-1}\,mol^{-1}}$
- **(2)** $8.0\,\mathrm{JK^{-1}\,mol^{-1}}$
- (3) $7.5\,\mathrm{JK^{-1}\,mol^{-1}}$
- (4) $7.0\,\mathrm{JK^{-1}\,mol^{-1}}$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. No. of mole of gas $= 1$ so molar mass $= 4\,\mathrm{g/mole}$
2. $V = \sqrt{\frac{\gamma RT}{m}} \Rightarrow 952 \times 952 = \frac{\gamma \times 3.3 \times 273}{4 \times 10^{-3}} \Rightarrow \gamma = 1.6 = \frac{16}{10} = \frac{8}{5}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. At NTP $22.4\,\mathrm{L}$ is one mole, so $M = 4.0\,\mathrm{g\,mol^{-1}} = 4\times10^{-3}\,\mathrm{kg\,mol^{-1}}$.
2. Speed of sound: $v = \sqrt{\frac{\gamma R T}{M}}$, so $\gamma = \frac{v^2 M}{R T}$.
3. $\gamma = \frac{(952)^2 \times 4\times10^{-3}}{8.3 \times 273} = \frac{3625.216}{2265.9} = 1.6$.
4. $C_P = \gamma C_V = 1.6 \times 5.0 = 8.0\,\mathrm{JK^{-1}\,mol^{-1}}$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Speed of Sound and Its Variation · **Difficulty:** hard (4 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 47)

![printed question 161](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q161_34b304ad_28102026.png?v=34b304adaa61)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 161](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q161_2e29dc47_28102026.png?v=2e29dc47499c)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** The speed of sound in a gas measures $\gamma$, and $\gamma = C_p/C_v$ turns the given $C_v$ into $C_p$
- **Trap:** **Condition dropped** (`condition_missed`) — drop the condition the stem states and the option becomes true — option 4  
  With $C_p = \gamma C_v$ in hand, the only missing number is $\gamma$, and the stored value $1.4$ is reached for instead of measured, giving exactly $1.4 \times 5.0 = 7.0$. That value belongs to a diatomic gas, and the stem's own speed of sound fixes $\gamma = v^2 M / RT = 1.6$ here.
- **Why:** This question requires the speed of sound to be converted into $\gamma$ before $C_p = \gamma C_v$ can be used, demonstrated by the stem supplying 'the speed of sound in this gas at NTP is $952\,\mathrm{ms^{-1}}$' alongside $C_v$ and $R$.
- **Note:** The printed data are not self-consistent for an ideal gas: $C_v = 5.0$ is below $\frac{3}{2}R$, and the keyed $C_p - C_v = 8.0 - 5.0 = 3.0$ is not $R = 8.3$, so Mayer's relation cannot be the intended route. Tagged as printed; the trap does not depend on this.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q162

Physics · Alternating Current

A series R-C circuit is connected to an alternating voltage source. Consider two situations: Current through resistor is $i$ and voltage across capacitor is $V$ then:

- **(i)** When capacitor is air filled.
- **(ii)** When capacitor is mica filled.

- (1) $V_a = V_b$
- (2) $V_a < V_b$
- **(3)** $V_a > V_b$
- (4) $i_a > i_b$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: Series R-C circuit with an AC source](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q162_sol_1_85cf804a_28102026.png?v=85cf804ab441)

*Series R-C circuit with an AC source*

1. $i = \frac{v}{\sqrt{R^{2} + \left(\frac{1}{c\omega}\right)^{2}}}$
2. $V_{C} = \frac{v}{\sqrt{R^{2} + \left(\frac{1}{c\omega}\right)^{2}}} \times \left(\frac{1}{c\omega}\right)$
3. $V_{C} = \frac{V}{\sqrt{(Rc\omega)^{2} + 1}}$
4. If we fill a di-electric material
5. $C \uparrow \Rightarrow V_{C} \downarrow$
6. Ans is (3)

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Filling the capacitor with mica (dielectric constant $K>1$) gives $C_b = K C_a > C_a$.
2. Capacitive reactance $X_C = \frac{1}{\omega C}$, so $X_{C,b} < X_{C,a}$.
3. Current $i = \frac{V_0}{\sqrt{R^2 + X_C^2}}$, so $i_b > i_a$; option $4$ ($i_a > i_b$) is false.
4. Voltage across capacitor $V = i X_C = \frac{V_0}{\sqrt{(R/X_C)^2 + 1}}$, which decreases as $X_C$ decreases.
5. Hence $V_a > V_b$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** RC Circuit · **Difficulty:** hard (4 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 47)

![printed question 162](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q162_6be4be77_28102026.png?v=6be4be7782a4)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 162](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q162_56feb1cd_28102026.png?v=56feb1cd206a)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** A dielectric raises $C$, which lowers $X_C = 1/(\omega C)$, so in a series R-C circuit the current rises and the capacitor's share of the source voltage falls
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 2  
  Mica raises $C$, so $X_C$ falls, the impedance falls and the current rises; a student who reads $V = i X_C$ and follows only the rising current puts the bigger capacitor voltage on the mica case, when $X_C$ has fallen faster and $V$ drops.
- **Why:** This question requires the reactance relation $X_C = 1/(\omega C)$ applied to a changed dielectric with no quantity produced, demonstrated by the stem's two situations 'When capacitor is air filled' and 'When capacitor is mica filled' and by options that are nothing but inequalities such as '$V_a > V_b$'.
- **Note:** Shape: the record prints a two-item lettered block (i, ii) while the options carry subscripts a and b, so the mapping rests on the printed order (a = air filled, b = mica filled). The two lettered items are the experimental situations being compared, not claims graded for truth and not a pairing, so qtype is mcq rather than statements; E1's plain_mcq (default, no pattern fired) is consistent with the printed shape. The i/ii against a/b relabelling is a storage inconsistency, not a loss of content - no stem value, option or figure is missing - so the record is tagged as printed rather than as source damage. Key agrees with the physics as printed: larger $C$ gives smaller $X_C$ and a smaller capacitor voltage, so $V_a > V_b$. Option 4's inequality is also an inverted direction ($i_a < i_b$ in fact), so the paper prints two reversals; the single trap finding is placed on option 2 because it is the exact inverse of the keyed inequality and so sits on the same quantity the stem keys on.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q163

Physics · Laws of Motion

A plank with a box on it at one end is gradually raised about the other end. As the angle of inclination with the horizontal reaches $30^{\circ}$, the box starts to slip and slides $4.0\,\mathrm{m}$ down the plank in $4.0\,\mathrm{s}$. The coefficients of static and kinetic friction between the box and the plank will be, respectively:

![printed figure: A box on a plank inclined at angle theta to the horizontal, with its weight mg and direction of sliding](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q163_4e9a5cc7_28102026.png?v=4e9a5cc76a47)

*A box on a plank inclined at angle theta to the horizontal, with its weight mg and direction of sliding*

- (1) $0.4$ and $0.3$
- (2) $0.6$ and $0.6$
- **(3)** $0.6$ and $0.5$
- (4) $0.5$ and $0.6$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mu_s = \tan 30^{\circ} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} = 0.5$
2. $\mu_s = 0.57 = 0.6$
3. $S = ut + \frac{1}{2}at^{2}$
4. $4 = \frac{1}{2}a(4)^{2} \Rightarrow a = \frac{1}{2} = 0.5$
5. $a = g\sin\theta - \mu_k(g)\cos\theta$
6. $\Rightarrow \mu_K = \frac{0.9}{\sqrt{3}} = 0.5$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. At the angle where slipping just begins, $\mu_s = \tan 30^{\circ} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{3}} \approx 0.577 \approx 0.6$.
2. Starting from rest, $s = \frac{1}{2} a t^2$, so $4.0 = \frac{1}{2} a (4.0)^2$.
3. $a = \frac{2 \times 4.0}{16} = 0.5\,\mathrm{m\,s^{-2}}$.
4. Down the incline while sliding: $a = g(\sin 30^{\circ} - \mu_k \cos 30^{\circ})$.
5. $0.5 = 10(0.5 - 0.866\,\mu_k) \Rightarrow 0.866\,\mu_k = 0.45$.
6. $\mu_k = \frac{0.45}{0.866} \approx 0.52 \approx 0.5$.
7. $\mu_s \approx 0.6$ and $\mu_k \approx 0.5$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Static Friction, Kinetic Friction · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 48)

![printed question 163](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q163_ffd734af_28102026.png?v=ffd734af4932)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 163](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q163_669bc2af_28102026.png?v=669bc2afd06e)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** The angle at which sliding begins gives $\mu_s = \tan\theta$, while the acceleration measured during the slide gives $\mu_k$
- **Trap:** **Partly correct** (`half_right`) — one part of the option is right and one part is wrong — option 2  
  The first number is got correctly from the slipping angle, $\mu_s = \tan 30^{\circ} = 0.6$, and the second is then produced without touching the slide data at all. The slide is accelerated, $a = 2s/t^2 = 0.5\,\mathrm{ms^{-2}}$, so $\mu_k = (g\sin\theta - a)/(g\cos\theta) = 0.5$ and only the first half of this pair holds.
- **Why:** This question requires two coefficients computed from two separate measurements, demonstrated by the stem giving both the angle at which 'the box starts to slip' and the '$4.0\,\mathrm{m}$ down the plank in $4.0\,\mathrm{s}$' slide.
- **Note:** The figure shows only the plank, the box and $mg$; every number needed is in the text, so the figure illustrates rather than decides.

## Q164

Physics · Laws of Motion

Two stones of masses $ma$ and $2\,m$ are whirled in horizontal circles, the heavier one in a radius $\frac{r}{2}$ and the lighter one in radius $r$. the tangential speed of lighter stone is $n$ times that of the value of heavier stone when they experience same centripetal forces. The value of $n$ is:

- (1) $1$
- **(2)** $2$
- (3) $3$
- (4) $4$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $F_{C} = \frac{mv_{1}^{2}}{r} = \frac{2mv_{2}^{2}}{\left(\frac{r}{2}\right)} = \frac{4mv_{2}^{2}}{r}$
2. So $V_{1} = 2V_{2}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Centripetal force on the lighter stone: $F_1 = \frac{m v_1^2}{r}$.
2. Centripetal force on the heavier stone: $F_2 = \frac{(2m) v_2^2}{r/2} = \frac{4 m v_2^2}{r}$.
3. Set $F_1 = F_2$: $\frac{m v_1^2}{r} = \frac{4 m v_2^2}{r} \Rightarrow v_1^2 = 4 v_2^2$.
4. $n = \frac{v_1}{v_2} = 2$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Centripetal and Centrifugal Force · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 49)

![printed question 164](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q164_1e50fed4_28102026.png?v=1e50fed4328e)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 164](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q164_39ddcff4_28102026.png?v=39ddcff4ed3b)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Proportionality** (`relation`) — how much one quantity changes when another does
- **Concept:** At equal centripetal force $mv^2/r$, speed scales as $\sqrt{Fr/m}$, so the mass and radius ratios fix the speed ratio
- **Trap:** **Arithmetic slip** (`arithmetic_slip`) — the number or the unit is wrong, or a step was stopped short — option 4  
  The two forces are equated correctly and the algebra ends at $v_l^2 = 4v_h^2$, which is then reported as the answer. The question asks how many times one SPEED is of the other, so the square root is still owed and $n = 2$.
- **Why:** This question requires how many times one speed is of the other at a fixed centripetal force, demonstrated by the stem's 'the tangential speed of lighter stone is $n$ times that of the ... heavier stone' and options that are bare factors 1, 2, 3, 4.
- **Note:** The stem prints the lighter mass as '$ma$', evidently $m$; the mass ratio $m : 2m$ is clear from the rest of the sentence and the tagging does not turn on it.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q165

Physics · Thermodynamics

The coefficient of performance of a refrigerator is 5. If the temperature inside freezer is $-20^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$, the temperature of the surrounding to which is rejects heat is:

- (1) $21^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$
- **(2)** $31^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$
- (3) $41^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$
- (4) $11^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: Refrigerator: heat q2 taken from cold reservoir TC, work input, heat q1 rejected to hot reservoir TH](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q165_sol_1_c535fab7_28102026.png?v=c535fab76862)

*Refrigerator: heat q2 taken from cold reservoir TC, work input, heat q1 rejected to hot reservoir TH*

1. $\mathrm{cop} = \frac{q_1}{w} = \frac{q_2}{q_1 - q_2} = \frac{T_c}{T_H - T_C} = 5$
2. $T_C = 5T_H - 5T_c$
3. $6T_c = 5T_H$
4. $T_H = \frac{6}{5} \times 253\,\mathrm{k} = 303.6\,\mathrm{k} = 30.6^{\circ}\mathrm{C} = 31^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Convert the freezer temperature to kelvin: $T_L = -20 + 273 = 253\,\mathrm{K}$.
2. For a refrigerator, $\beta = \frac{T_L}{T_H - T_L} = 5$.
3. $T_H - T_L = \frac{253}{5} = 50.6\,\mathrm{K}$.
4. $T_H = 253 + 50.6 = 303.6\,\mathrm{K}$.
5. $303.6 - 273 = 30.6^{\circ}\mathrm{C} \approx 31^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Refrigerator and Heat Pump · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 49)

![printed question 165](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q165_0e77d694_28102026.png?v=0e77d694077f)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 165](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q165_7a4a8923_28102026.png?v=7a4a8923f356)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** A refrigerator's ideal coefficient of performance is $T_{\mathrm{cold}}/(T_{\mathrm{hot}} - T_{\mathrm{cold}})$, with both temperatures in kelvin
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 3  
  One coefficient-of-performance ratio is stored without which temperature sits on top, and the hot one is chosen because the stem talks about rejecting heat, giving $T_h/(T_h - T_c) = 5$ and $T_h = 316\,\mathrm{K} = 43^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$. A refrigerator is rated on the heat pulled from the cold space, $T_c/(T_h - T_c) = 5$, which gives $T_h = 304\,\mathrm{K} = 31^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$.
- **Why:** This question requires the refrigerator's coefficient of performance to be inverted for the hot-reservoir temperature in kelvin, demonstrated by the stem giving 'the coefficient of performance of a refrigerator is 5' and 'the temperature inside freezer is $-20^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$' and asking for the surrounding temperature.
- **Note:** The options are a $10^{\circ}$ ladder, so the two misplaced-formula routes land near $41^{\circ}$ and $21^{\circ}$ rather than exactly on them; the arithmetic of each route is given in full so a reviewer can check the fit. The stem's 'to which is rejects heat' is a typo carried from the source.

## Q166

Physics · Thermodynamics

An ideal gas is compressed to half its initial volume by means of several processes. Which of the process results in the maximum work done on the gas?

- (1) Isothermal
- **(2)** Adiabatic
- (3) Isobaric
- (4) Isochoric

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: P-V diagram: adiabatic, isothermal and isobaric compressions to half volume](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q166_sol_1_71d8d70a_28102026.png?v=71d8d70a1884)

*P-V diagram: adiabatic, isothermal and isobaric compressions to half volume*

1. Since area under the curve is max for adiabatic process so work done on the gas will be max for adiabatic process.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Work done on the gas is $W_{on} = -\int_{V_0}^{V_0/2} P\,dV$, the area under the $P$-$V$ curve between $V_0/2$ and $V_0$.
2. Isochoric: $dV = 0$, so $W = 0$; the volume cannot even halve.
3. Isobaric: $P = P_0$ throughout, so $W = P_0 \frac{V_0}{2}$.
4. Isothermal: $P = P_0 \frac{V_0}{V}$, so $W = P_0 V_0 \ln 2 \approx 0.69\,P_0 V_0 > 0.5\,P_0 V_0$.
5. Adiabatic: $P = P_0 \left(\frac{V_0}{V}\right)^{\gamma}$ with $\gamma > 1$, which lies above the isothermal curve for $V < V_0$, so its area is the largest.
6. Hence $W_{adiabatic} > W_{isothermal} > W_{isobaric} > W_{isochoric}$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Adiabatic Process, Thermodynamic Processes · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 50)

![printed question 166](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q166_6e2c541d_28102026.png?v=6e2c541d95a3)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 166](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q166_607e4994_28102026.png?v=607e49947215)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Comparison** (`compare`) — the difference between two things, or the correct pairing
- **Concept:** On a $P$-$V$ diagram the adiabat is steeper than the isotherm, so compressing a gas along it costs the most work
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 1  
  The remembered ranking is 'isothermal work beats adiabatic work', which holds for EXPANSION, where the isotherm lies above the adiabat. Flip the process to a compression and the ranking flips with it: $0.80\,nRT$ of work goes in adiabatically against $0.69\,nRT$ isothermally.
- **Why:** This question requires four named processes to be ranked by the area under their $P$-$V$ curves for one and the same volume change, demonstrated by the stem asking 'which of the process results in the maximum work done on the gas' with four bare process names as options.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q167

Physics · Work, Energy and Power

A ball is thrown vertically downwards from a height of $20\,\mathrm{m}$ with an initial velocity $v_0$. It collides with the ground, loses 50 percent of its energy in collision and rebounds to the same height. The initial velocity $v_0$ is: (Take $g = 10\,\mathrm{ms^{-2}}$)

- (1) $10\,ms^{-1}$
- (2) $14\,ms^{-1}$
- **(3)** $20\,ms^{-1}$
- (4) $28\,ms^{-1}$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: A ball M falling from height h onto the ground, shown at the top and just above the ground](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q167_sol_1_19a21f98_28102026.png?v=19a21f98ff28)

*A ball M falling from height h onto the ground, shown at the top and just above the ground*

1. $\frac{KE_{f}}{KE_{i}} = \frac{1}{2}$
2. $\frac{V_{f}}{V_{i}} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}$
3. $\frac{\sqrt{2gh}}{\sqrt{V_{0}^{2} + 2gh}} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}$
4. $V_{0} = 20\,\mathrm{m/sec}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. To rise back to $h = 20\,\mathrm{m}$ the ball needs kinetic energy $\frac{1}{2}mv'^2 = mgh = m(10)(20) = 200m$ just after the collision.
2. It lost $50\%$ of its energy, so just before impact $\frac{1}{2}mv^2 = 2 \times 200m = 400m$, giving $v^2 = 800\,\mathrm{m^2s^{-2}}$.
3. For the downward fall, $v^2 = v_0^2 + 2gh = v_0^2 + 2(10)(20) = v_0^2 + 400$.
4. $v_0^2 = 800 - 400 = 400$, so $v_0 = 20\,\mathrm{ms^{-1}}$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Energy Conservation · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 50)

![printed question 167](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q167_d361100e_28102026.png?v=d361100e6b40)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 167](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q167_41ba1651_28102026.png?v=41ba16515803)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Mechanical energy conservation across a lossy bounce: the ball arrives with $\frac{1}{2}mv_0^2+mgh$, keeps half of it, and that half must equal $mgh$ to climb back to 20 m
- **Trap:** **Answers another question** (`answers_other_question`) — true, and about this topic, but it answers something else — option 4  
  $28\,\mathrm{ms^{-1}}$ is the true speed the ball arrives at the ground with, $\sqrt{v_0^2+2gh}=\sqrt{800}=28.3$, so a student working backwards from the rebound reaches a printed number one step before the one asked for.
- **Why:** This question requires an energy balance taken across a collision that keeps half the energy, demonstrated by "loses 50 percent of its energy in collision and rebounds to the same height" with four speeds as the options.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q168

Physics · Work, Energy and Power

On a frictionless surfaces, a block of mass $M$ moving at speed $v$ collides elastically with another block of same mass $M$ which is initially at rest. After collision the first block moves at an angle $\theta$ to its initial direction and has a speed $\frac{v}{3}$. The second block’s speed after the collision is:

- (1) $\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}v$
- **(2)** $\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{3}v$
- (3) $\frac{3}{4}v$
- (4) $\frac{3}{\sqrt{2}}v$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: Block M moving at V hits block M at rest; afterwards the two blocks move off at 90 degrees, one at V/3](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q168_sol_1_f193c480_28102026.png?v=f193c4804034)

*Block M moving at V hits block M at rest; afterwards the two blocks move off at 90 degrees, one at V/3*

1. $\vec{P_i} = \vec{P_f}$
2. $\Rightarrow |P_i| = |P_f| \Rightarrow \sqrt{\left(m\frac{V}{3}\right)^{2} + (mV_2)^{2}}$
3. $V_2 = \frac{2\sqrt{2}}{3}V$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The collision is elastic, so kinetic energy is conserved: $\frac{1}{2}Mv^2 = \frac{1}{2}M\left(\frac{v}{3}\right)^2 + \frac{1}{2}Mv_2^2$.
2. Cancel $\frac{1}{2}M$: $v^2 = \frac{v^2}{9} + v_2^2$.
3. $v_2^2 = v^2 - \frac{v^2}{9} = \frac{8v^2}{9}$.
4. $v_2 = \frac{2\sqrt{2}}{3}v$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Problems on Elastic Collision · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 51)

![printed question 168](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q168_1075b4f8_28102026.png?v=1075b4f8fdce)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 168](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q168_5648e35d_28102026.png?v=5648e35d6b4b)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** An elastic collision conserves kinetic energy, so for two equal masses the outgoing speeds obey $v^2=v_1^2+v_2^2$
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 1  
  A student who carries "equal masses share the speed" substitutes $\frac{v}{2}$ where the stem prints $\frac{v}{3}$, and the energy equation then returns $\sqrt{v^2-\frac{v^2}{4}}=\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}v$.
- **Why:** This question requires kinetic energy conservation applied to two equal masses, demonstrated by "collides elastically with another block of same mass" together with the printed outgoing speed $\frac{v}{3}$.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q169

Physics · Electrostatics

If potential (in volts) in a region is expressed as $V(x, y, z) = 6xy - y + 2yz$, the electric field (in N/C) at point $(1, 1, 0)$ is:

- (1) $-(6\hat{i} + 9\hat{j} + \hat{k})$
- (2) $-(3\hat{i} + 5\hat{j} + 3\hat{k})$
- **(3)** $-(6\hat{i} + 5\hat{j} + 2\hat{k})$
- (4) $-(2\hat{i} + 3\hat{j} + \hat{k})$

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $V = 6xy - y + 24z$
2. $\bar{E} = \left(\frac{\partial V}{\partial x}\hat{i} + \frac{\partial V}{\partial y}\hat{j}\,\frac{\partial V}{\partial z}\hat{k}\right)$
3. $\bar{E} = \left[(6y)\hat{i} + (6x - 1 + 2z)\hat{j} + (2y)\hat{k}\right]$
4. $\bar{E} = -\left(6\hat{i} + 5\hat{j} + 2\hat{k}\right)$
5. $(1, 1, 0)$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The field is the negative gradient of the potential: $\vec{E} = -\left(\frac{\partial V}{\partial x}\hat{i} + \frac{\partial V}{\partial y}\hat{j} + \frac{\partial V}{\partial z}\hat{k}\right)$.
2. $\frac{\partial V}{\partial x} = 6y = 6$ at $(1,1,0)$.
3. $\frac{\partial V}{\partial y} = 6x - 1 + 2z = 6 - 1 + 0 = 5$ at $(1,1,0)$.
4. $\frac{\partial V}{\partial z} = 2y = 2$ at $(1,1,0)$.
5. $\vec{E} = -(6\hat{i} + 5\hat{j} + 2\hat{k})\,\text{N/C}$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Relation Between Field and Potential · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 51)

![printed question 169](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q169_f76b7e7b_28102026.png?v=f76b7e7b5b4d)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 169](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q169_99b0199f_28102026.png?v=99b0199f3391)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** The electric field is minus the gradient of the potential: one partial derivative per component, each evaluated at the given point
- **Trap:** **No trap** (`no_trap`) — nothing pulls; the other options are only filler  
  Every single-slip value this potential can yield was carried through: dropping the $-y$ term gives $-(6\hat{i}+6\hat{j}+2\hat{k})$, flipping that term's sign gives $-(6\hat{i}+7\hat{j}+2\hat{k})$, and reading $z=1$ instead of $0$ also gives $-(6\hat{i}+7\hat{j}+2\hat{k})$. Neither appears among the options, and none of the three wrong options is reached by a single operation on $6xy-y+2yz$.
- **Why:** This question requires three partial derivatives of a printed potential evaluated at one point, demonstrated by "$V(x, y, z) = 6xy - y + 2yz$" and "the electric field (in N/C) at point $(1, 1, 0)$".
- **Note:** The three wrong options are a numeric spread rather than routes: each reproduces at most one component of $-(6\hat{i}+5\hat{j}+2\hat{k})$, and the two values the natural slips actually produce, $(6,6,2)$ and $(6,7,2)$, are not printed. Three fillers is recorded here as the finding after that enumeration, not as unfinished work.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q170

Physics · Wave Optics

Two slits in Young’s experiment have widths in the ratio $1:25$. The ratio of intensity at the maxima and minima in the interference pattern, $\frac{I_{\mathrm{max}}}{I_{\mathrm{min}}}$ is:

- (1) $\frac{4}{9}$
- **(2)** $\frac{9}{4}$
- (3) $\frac{121}{49}$
- (4) $\frac{49}{121}$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\frac{\mathrm{I}_1}{\mathrm{I}_2} = \frac{25}{1} \Rightarrow \frac{\mathrm{A}_1}{\mathrm{A}_2} = \frac{5}{1}$
2. $\frac{\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{max}}}{\mathrm{A}_{\mathrm{min}}} = \frac{5 + 1}{5 - 1} = \frac{6}{4} = \frac{3}{2}$
3. $\frac{\mathrm{I}_{\mathrm{max}}}{\mathrm{I}_{\mathrm{min}}} = \left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{2} = \frac{9}{4}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Intensity from a slit is proportional to its width, so $\frac{I_1}{I_2} = \frac{1}{25}$.
2. Amplitude goes as the square root of intensity, so $\frac{a_1}{a_2} = \sqrt{\frac{1}{25}} = \frac{1}{5}$.
3. $\frac{I_{\mathrm{max}}}{I_{\mathrm{min}}} = \frac{(a_2 + a_1)^2}{(a_2 - a_1)^2} = \frac{(5+1)^2}{(5-1)^2}$.
4. $\frac{36}{16} = \frac{9}{4}$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Intensity Variation · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 52)

![printed question 170](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q170_4944c080_28102026.png?v=4944c08016fd)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 170](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q170_543415e1_28102026.png?v=543415e19f8f)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Slit width sets intensity and intensity sets amplitude, so the fringe contrast is $\left(\frac{a_1+a_2}{a_1-a_2}\right)^2$
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 1  
  $\frac{4}{9}$ is $\frac{I_{\mathrm{min}}}{I_{\mathrm{max}}}$: a student who builds the quotient with the difference of amplitudes on top gets $\frac{16}{36}=\frac{4}{9}$ and finds it printed.
- **Why:** This question requires the width-to-intensity-to-amplitude chain fed into the fringe-contrast formula, demonstrated by "widths in the ratio $1:25$" and the asked "$\frac{I_{\mathrm{max}}}{I_{\mathrm{min}}}$".
<!--/ecat-->

## Q171

Physics · Work, Energy and Power

The heart of a man pumps $5$ litres of blood through the arteries per minute at a pressure of $150\,\mathrm{mm}$ of mercury. If the density of mercury be $13.6 \times 10^{3}\,\mathrm{kg/m^{3}}$ and $g = 10\,\mathrm{m/s^{2}}$ then the power of heart in watt is:

- (1) $1.50$
- **(2)** $1.70$
- (3) $2.35$
- (4) $3.0$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Power $= \vec{F}\,.\,\vec{V} = P A \vec{V} = \rho g h\, A V$
2. $= 13.6 \times 10^{3} \times 10 \times 150 \times 10^{-3} \times 0.5 \times 10^{-3} / 60$ watt
3. $= \frac{102}{60}$ watt $= 1.70$ watt.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Pressure of $150\,\mathrm{mm}$ of mercury: $p = h\rho g = 0.150 \times 13.6\times10^{3} \times 10 = 2.04\times10^{4}\,\mathrm{Pa}$.
2. Volume flow rate: $Q = \frac{5\times10^{-3}\,\mathrm{m^3}}{60\,\mathrm{s}} = 8.33\times10^{-5}\,\mathrm{m^3/s}$.
3. Power of heart: $P = pQ = 2.04\times10^{4} \times 8.33\times10^{-5} = 1.70\,\mathrm{W}$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Power · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 52)

![printed question 171](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q171_6036b99d_28102026.png?v=6036b99db428)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 171](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q171_6c93263b_28102026.png?v=6c93263b4c11)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** A pump's power is pressure times volume flow rate, with the pressure read off a mercury column as $\rho g h$
- **Trap:** **No trap** (`no_trap`) — nothing pulls; the other options are only filler  
  Each slip in this question's unit chain was carried through to a number: leaving $h$ in millimetres gives $1700$ W, reading the flow as 5 litres per second gives $102$ W, dropping $g$ gives $0.17$ W, and using $g=9.8$ gives $1.67$ W. None of $1.50$, $2.35$ or $3.0$ is any of these, so no wrong option sits on a route through the work this question asks for.
- **Why:** This question requires a pressure built from a mercury column multiplied by a volume flow rate, demonstrated by "at a pressure of $150\,\mathrm{mm}$ of mercury" with "$5$ litres of blood through the arteries per minute" and watts in the options.
- **Note:** The work in this question is the mm-to-m, litre-to-m³ and minute-to-second chain, and no distractor sits on a slip in that chain; the three wrong options are a numeric spread around $1.70$ W. Three fillers is recorded as the finding after enumerating the chain, not for want of looking.

## Q172

Physics · Magnetic Effect of Electric Current

A proton and an alpha particle both enter a region of uniform magnetic field $B$, moving at right angles to the field $B$. if the radius of circular orbits for both the particles is equal and the kinetic energy acquired by proton is $1\,\mathrm{MeV}$, the energy acquired by the alpha particle will be:

- **(1)** $1\,\mathrm{MeV}$
- (2) $4\,\mathrm{MeV}$
- (3) $0.5\,\mathrm{MeV}$
- (4) $1.5\,\mathrm{MeV}$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\mathrm{R} = \frac{\mathrm{mV}}{\mathrm{qB}} = \frac{\sqrt{2\mathrm{m}(\mathrm{kE})}}{\mathrm{qB}}$
2. Since $\mathrm{R}$ is same so $\mathrm{KE} \propto \frac{\mathrm{q}^{2}}{\mathrm{m}}$
3. So $\mathrm{KE}$ of $\alpha$ particle will be $\frac{(2)^{2}}{4} = \text{same} = 1\,\mathrm{MeV}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. For circular motion in the field, $qvB = \frac{mv^2}{r}$, so $r = \frac{mv}{qB} = \frac{\sqrt{2mK}}{qB}$.
2. Rearranging, $K = \frac{q^2 B^2 r^2}{2m}$; with equal $B$ and $r$, $K \propto \frac{q^2}{m}$.
3. For the alpha particle $q_\alpha = 2e$ and $m_\alpha = 4m_p$, so $\frac{K_\alpha}{K_p} = \left(\frac{2e}{e}\right)^2 \frac{m_p}{4m_p} = 4 \times \frac{1}{4} = 1$.
4. $K_\alpha = 1 \times 1\,\mathrm{MeV} = 1\,\mathrm{MeV}$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Motion in a Magnetic Field · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 52)

![printed question 172](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q172_8bb65f38_28102026.png?v=8bb65f3881f4)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 172](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q172_09fbcfd4_28102026.png?v=09fbcfd4f709)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Proportionality** (`relation`) — how much one quantity changes when another does
- **Concept:** At a fixed orbit radius in a given magnetic field the kinetic energy goes as $\frac{q^2}{m}$, and for an alpha particle both the squared charge and the mass rise fourfold and cancel
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 2  
  Holding $r=\frac{\sqrt{2mK}}{qB}$ fixed while remembering only that the alpha carries twice the charge turns the relation into $K\propto q^2$, which gives $4$ MeV; the $\frac{1}{m}$ sitting in the same formula, and changing by the same factor of four, is left out.
- **Why:** This question requires how kinetic energy scales with charge and mass when the orbit radius is held fixed, demonstrated by "the radius of circular orbits for both the particles is equal" with the proton's energy given as $1\,\mathrm{MeV}$.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q173

Physics · Semiconductor Electronics

The input signal given to a CE amplifier having a voltage gain of 150 is $V_i = 2\cos\left(15t + \frac{\pi}{3}\right)$. The corresponding output signal will be:

- **(1)** $300\cos\left(15t + \frac{4\pi}{3}\right)$
- (2) $300\cos\left(15t + \frac{\pi}{3}\right)$
- (3) $75\cos\left(15t + \frac{2\pi}{3}\right)$
- (4) $2\cos\left(15t + \frac{5\pi}{6}\right)$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. CE amplifier causes phase difference of $\pi\,(= 180^{\circ})$ so $V_{\mathrm{out}} = 300\cos\left(15t + \frac{\pi}{3} + \pi\right)$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The output amplitude is the gain times the input amplitude: $A_v V_0 = 150 \times 2 = 300$.
2. A CE amplifier gives a phase shift of $\pi$ between input and output, so the output phase is $\frac{\pi}{3} + \pi = \frac{4\pi}{3}$.
3. $V_o = 300\cos\left(15t + \frac{4\pi}{3}\right)$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Transistor as an Amplifier (Common Emitter Configuration): Voltage, Current and Power Gain · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 53)

![printed question 173](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q173_75980fe9_28102026.png?v=75980fe944d6)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 173](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q173_e8fe54a1_28102026.png?v=e8fe54a147cc)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** A common-emitter amplifier multiplies the input amplitude by its voltage gain and turns the signal through $180^\circ$
- **Trap:** **Partly correct** (`half_right`) — one part of the option is right and one part is wrong — option 2  
  The gain relation $V_o = A_vV_i$ settles the amplitude at once, $2\times150 = 300$, and the phase is then copied across from the input unchanged because the stem never mentions phase.
- **Why:** This question requires the voltage-gain relation and the common-emitter phase inversion to be applied to a given input, demonstrated by the stem's 'voltage gain of 150' with '$V_i = 2\cos(15t+\frac{\pi}{3})$' and options that differ in amplitude and in phase.

## Q174

Physics · Units and Measurements

If dimensions of critical velocity $v_c$ of a liquid flowing through a tube are expressed as $[\eta^{x}\rho^{y}r^{z}]$, where $\eta$, $\rho$ and $r$ are the coefficient of viscosity of liquid, density of liquid and radius of the tube respectively, then the values of $x$, $y$ and $z$ are given by:

- (1) $1, 1, 1$
- **(2)** $1, -1, -1$
- (3) $-1, -1, 1$
- (4) $-1, -1, -1$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $V_c = \eta^{x}\rho^{y}r^{z}$
2. Critical velocity is given by $V_c = \frac{R\eta}{2\rho r}$
3. So, $x = 1$
4. $y = -1$
5. $z = -1$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Write dimensions: $[\eta] = [ML^{-1}T^{-1}]$, $[\rho] = [ML^{-3}]$, $[r] = [L]$, $[v_c] = [LT^{-1}]$.
2. $[LT^{-1}] = [M^{x+y} L^{-x-3y+z} T^{-x}]$.
3. Compare $T$: $-x = -1 \Rightarrow x = 1$.
4. Compare $M$: $x + y = 0 \Rightarrow y = -1$.
5. Compare $L$: $-x - 3y + z = 1 \Rightarrow -1 + 3 + z = 1 \Rightarrow z = -1$.
6. $x, y, z = 1, -1, -1$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Deducing Relation Among the Physical Quantities Using Dimensional Equation · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 53)

![printed question 174](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q174_d7500935_28102026.png?v=d7500935d166)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 174](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q174_ca6a4445_28102026.png?v=ca6a4445d03e)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **Two answers** (`two_part`) — the answer has two halves, usually joined by 'respectively'
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Fixing the exponents in a product of quantities by matching the dimensions of mass, length and time on the two sides
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 4  
  Critical velocity is recalled as falling with radius and with density, and viscosity is then put underneath as well on the sense that a thicker liquid flows more slowly, giving $\eta^{-1}\rho^{-1}r^{-1}$.
- **Why:** This question requires the exponents of a product of quantities to be fixed by dimensional matching, demonstrated by the stem's 'dimensions of critical velocity $v_c$ ... are expressed as $[\eta^{x}\rho^{y}r^{z}]$' with options that are triples of $\pm1$.
- **Note:** E1 returned the retired label `multi_part_respectively`; recorded as `two_part` because each option is a triple of exponents accepted or rejected whole, which is the nearest current shape.

## Q175

Physics · Current Electricity

A circuit contains an ammeter, a battery of $30\,\mathrm{v}$ and a resistance $40.8\,\mathrm{ohm}$ all connected in series. If the ammeter has coil of resistance $480\,\mathrm{ohm}$ and a shunt of $20\,\mathrm{ohm}$, the reading in the ammeter will be:

- (1) $1\,\mathrm{A}$
- **(2)** $0.5\,\mathrm{A}$
- (3) $0.25\,\mathrm{A}$
- (4) $2\,\mathrm{A}$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: Ammeter (coil S of 480 ohm with a 20 ohm shunt) in series with a 40.8 ohm resistor and a 30 v battery](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q175_sol_1_b5bd79e4_28102026.png?v=b5bd79e47635)

*Ammeter (coil S of 480 ohm with a 20 ohm shunt) in series with a 40.8 ohm resistor and a 30 v battery*

1. Resistance of ammeter $= \frac{480 \times 20}{480 + 20} = 19.2\,\Omega$
2. $i = \frac{30}{40.8 + 19.2} = 0.5\,\mathrm{A}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The coil and shunt are in parallel: $R_A = \frac{480 \times 20}{480 + 20} = \frac{9600}{500} = 19.2\,\Omega$.
2. Total series resistance: $R = 40.8 + 19.2 = 60\,\Omega$.
3. Current read by the ammeter: $I = \frac{V}{R} = \frac{30}{60} = 0.5\,\mathrm{A}$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Ammeter and Voltmeter in Circuits · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 53)

![printed question 175](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q175_bf5694eb_28102026.png?v=bf5694eb10c4)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 175](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q175_c9a15374_28102026.png?v=c9a153749c85)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** A shunted ammeter's own resistance is its coil and shunt in parallel, and the instrument reads the whole current passing through it
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 3  
  The line current $0.5\,\mathrm{A}$ is reached correctly and then halved, on the view that the coil and the shunt take one half each and that the needle follows the coil.
- **Why:** This question requires the ammeter's coil and shunt to be combined in parallel and then added in series before Ohm's law is applied, demonstrated by the stem's 'coil of resistance $480$ ohm and a shunt of $20$ ohm' together with 'all connected in series'.
- **Note:** The parallel step is weakly discriminated: treating the shunted ammeter as its $20\,\Omega$ shunt alone gives $\frac{30}{60.8} = 0.49\,\mathrm{A}$, which still lands on the printed key. Two of the four options also sit above the circuit's maximum possible current of $0.74\,\mathrm{A}$.

## Q176

Physics · Mechanical Properties of Fluids

Water rises to a height ‘$h$’ in capillary tube. If the length of capillary tube above the surface of water is made less than ‘$h$’, then:

- (1) Water does not rise at all
- (2) Water rises upto the tip of capillary tube and then starts overflowing like a fountain
- **(3)** Water rises upto the top of capillary tube and stays there without overflowing
- (4) Water rises upto a point a little below the top and stays there

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. Water will not overflow but will change its radius of curvature.

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Capillary rise obeys $h = \frac{2T\cos\theta}{r\rho g}$, i.e. $hR = \frac{2T}{\rho g}$ is constant, where $R = \frac{r}{\cos\theta}$ is the meniscus radius.
2. If the tube length $l < h$, water rises to the top and the meniscus radius increases to $R' = \frac{hR}{l}$ so that $lR' = hR$.
3. The meniscus flattens and the water stays at the top without overflowing (no perpetual fountain), which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Capillary Rise Problems · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 54)

![printed question 176](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q176_c72aec8f_28102026.png?v=c72aec8f9644)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 176](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q176_8b190695_28102026.png?v=8b19069556f7)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** Capillary rise is limited by the curvature the meniscus must have; a tube shorter than the rise flattens its meniscus instead of overflowing
- **Trap:** **Condition dropped** (`condition_missed`) — drop the condition the stem states and the option becomes true — option 2  
  The rise formula $h = \frac{2T\cos\theta}{r\rho g}$ is held as a height the water must arrive at, so with the tube ending below $h$ the surplus has to go somewhere and spills over the rim.
- **Why:** This question requires the capillary-rise relation to be applied in a case where its own condition fails, demonstrated by the stem's 'if the length of capillary tube above the surface of water is made less than $h$'.
- **Note:** E1 returned `statement_options`; recorded as `mcq` because no lettered statement block is printed and the stem does not ask statements to be judged — the options are simply long sentences.

## Q177

Physics · Ray Optics

In an astronomical telescope in normal adjustment a straight black line of the length $L$ is drawn on inside part of objective lens. The eye-piece forms a real image of this line. The length of this image is $I$. The magnification of the telescope is:

- **(1)** $\frac{L}{I}$
- (2) $\frac{\mathrm{L}}{\mathrm{I}}+1$
- (3) $\frac{\mathrm{L}}{\mathrm{I}}-1$
- (4) $\frac{\mathrm{L}+\mathrm{I}}{\mathrm{L}-\mathrm{I}}$

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: Objective lens with a line L drawn on it and the eye-piece a distance d = f0 + fe away](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q177_sol_1_10bb1aa0_28102026.png?v=10bb1aa0cc88)

*Objective lens with a line L drawn on it and the eye-piece a distance d = f0 + fe away*

1. Magnification by eyepiece
2. $m = \frac{f}{f+u}$
3. $-\frac{I}{L} = \frac{f_e}{f_e + (-(f_0 + f_e)}$
4. $\Rightarrow \frac{I}{L} = \frac{f_e}{f_0}$
5. $\mathrm{m.\,p.} = \frac{f_0}{f_e} = \frac{L}{I}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. In normal adjustment the objective-eyepiece separation is $f_o + f_e$, so the line is at $u = -(f_o + f_e)$ from the eyepiece.
2. Lens formula: $\frac{1}{v} - \frac{1}{u} = \frac{1}{f_e} \Rightarrow \frac{1}{v} = \frac{1}{f_e} - \frac{1}{f_o + f_e} = \frac{f_o}{f_e (f_o + f_e)}$.
3. So $v = \frac{f_e (f_o + f_e)}{f_o}$, a real image.
4. Linear magnification of this image: $\frac{I}{L} = \frac{v}{|u|} = \frac{f_e}{f_o}$.
5. Telescope magnification $M = \frac{f_o}{f_e} = \frac{L}{I}$, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Resolving and Magnifying Power of Telescope · **Difficulty:** hard (4 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 54)

![printed question 177](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q177_2796b43c_28102026.png?v=2796b43ceea3)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 177](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q177_5cb5c153_28102026.png?v=5cb5c1533fa5)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** In normal adjustment the telescope's magnification $f_o/f_e$ is also the ratio by which the eyepiece shrinks a mark drawn on the objective
- **Trap:** **Formula misused** (`formula_misused`) — the right formula with a wrong power, a dropped term or the wrong quantity — option 2  
  The eyepiece's object distance is taken as $f_o$ rather than the full separation $f_o+f_e$, and the lens equation then returns $\frac{I}{L} = \frac{f_e}{f_o-f_e}$, that is $\frac{f_o}{f_e} = \frac{L}{I}+1$.
- **Why:** This question requires the eyepiece's linear magnification of a mark on the objective to be identified with the telescope's magnification, demonstrated by 'a straight black line of the length $L$ is drawn on inside part of objective lens' and 'the eye-piece forms a real image of this line'.
<!--/ecat-->

## Q178

Physics · Thermal Properties of Matter

The value of coefficient of volume expansion of glycerin is $5 \times 10^{-4}\,\mathrm{K^{-1}}$. The fractional change in the density of glycerin for a rise of $40^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$ in its temperature is:

- (1) 0.010
- (2) 0.015
- **(3)** 0.020
- (4) 0.025

**Answer** — booklet: (3) · independent solve: (3) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $\rho = \rho_{0}(1 - \gamma\Delta t)$
2. $\frac{\Delta\rho}{\rho_{0}} = \gamma\Delta T = (5 \times 10^{-4})(40) = 0.02$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. Mass is constant, so $\rho V$ is constant and $\rho' = \frac{\rho}{1+\gamma\Delta T}$.
2. For small $\gamma\Delta T$, $\rho' \approx \rho(1-\gamma\Delta T)$, so $\left|\frac{\Delta\rho}{\rho}\right| = \gamma\Delta T$.
3. $\gamma\Delta T = 5\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{K^{-1}} \times 40\,\mathrm{K} = 0.020$, which is option $3$.

**Subtopic:** Thermal Expansion · **Difficulty:** medium (2 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 55)

![printed question 178](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q178_18ba245d_28102026.png?v=18ba245d49c0)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 178](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q178_2db9863d_28102026.png?v=2db9863d8571)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** A fractional change in density is the volume expansion read backwards: $\left|\frac{\Delta\rho}{\rho}\right| = \gamma\,\Delta T$
- **Trap:** **No trap** (`no_trap`) — nothing pulls; the other options are only filler  
  The three wrong options are a $0.005$ ladder around the answer rather than the output of any route: using the linear coefficient $\frac{\gamma}{3}$ gives $0.0067$, the exact form $\frac{\gamma\Delta T}{1+\gamma\Delta T}$ gives $0.0196$ which rounds onto the key itself, and reading the $40^\circ\mathrm{C}$ rise as an absolute $313\,\mathrm{K}$ gives $0.157$ — none of the three is printed.
- **Why:** This question requires one substitution into the volume-expansion relation, demonstrated by the stem supplying 'coefficient of volume expansion ... $5\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{K^{-1}}$' and 'a rise of $40^{\circ}\mathrm{C}$' and asking for 'the fractional change in the density'.
- **Note:** The options are separated only by steps of $0.005$ on a ladder through the answer. The nearest real wrong route, confusing the volume coefficient with the linear one, gives $0.0067$; $0.010$ is the closest printed value to that but is not what that route produces, so I have not tagged it as a trap.

## Q179

Physics · Modern Physics

A photoelectric surface is illuminated successively by monochromatic light of wavelength $\lambda$ and $\frac{\lambda}{2}$. If the maximum kinetic energy of the emitted photoelectrons in the second case is 3 times that in the first case, the work function of the surface of the material is:
(h = Planck's constant, c= speed of light)

- (1) $\frac{hc}{3\lambda}$
- **(2)** $\frac{hc}{2\lambda}$
- (3) $\frac{hc}{\lambda}$
- (4) $\frac{2hc}{\lambda}$

**Answer** — booklet: (2) · independent solve: (2) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

1. $k_1 = \frac{hc}{\lambda} - \psi$
2. $k_2 = 3k_1 = \frac{2hc}{\lambda} - \psi = \frac{3hc}{\lambda} - 3\psi$
3. So $2\psi = \frac{hc}{\lambda}$
4. So $\psi = \frac{hc}{2\lambda}$

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. First case: $K_1 = \frac{hc}{\lambda} - \phi$.
2. Second case: $K_2 = \frac{hc}{\lambda/2} - \phi = \frac{2hc}{\lambda} - \phi$.
3. Given $K_2 = 3K_1$: $\frac{2hc}{\lambda} - \phi = \frac{3hc}{\lambda} - 3\phi$.
4. $2\phi = \frac{hc}{\lambda}$, so $\phi = \frac{hc}{2\lambda}$, which is option $2$.

**Subtopic:** Photoelectric Effect · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 55)

![printed question 179](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q179_de9d2dae_28102026.png?v=de9d2daecdad)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 179](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q179_a568f343_28102026.png?v=a568f3432461)

---

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Calculation** (`calculate`) — put numbers in and get a number out
- **Concept:** Einstein's photoelectric equation: halving the wavelength doubles the photon energy while the work function stays fixed, so the two kinetic-energy equations solve for $\phi$
- **Trap:** **Arithmetic slip** (`arithmetic_slip`) — the number or the unit is wrong, or a step was stopped short — option 1  
  Gathering the work-function terms in $\frac{2hc}{\lambda}-\phi=3\left(\frac{hc}{\lambda}-\phi\right)$ as $3\phi=\frac{hc}{\lambda}$ instead of $2\phi=\frac{hc}{\lambda}$ carries the stem's factor 3 straight into the answer and gives $\frac{hc}{3\lambda}$.
- **Why:** This question requires Einstein's photoelectric equation written for both wavelengths with the kinetic energy eliminated between them, demonstrated by the stem supplying only a ratio, "the maximum kinetic energy of the emitted photoelectrons in the second case is 3 times that in the first case", and by all four options being expressions in $\frac{hc}{\lambda}$ rather than numbers.
- **Note:** The printed key (2) matches the algebra: $2\phi=\frac{hc}{\lambda}$, so $\phi=\frac{hc}{2\lambda}$. No option is left without a route, so no filler is recorded.

## Q180

Physics · Ray Optics

A beam of light consisting of red, green and blue colours is incident on a right angled prism. The refractive index of the material of the prism for the above red, green and blue wavelengths are 1.39,1.44 and 1.47 respectively. The prism will:

![printed figure: Blue, green and red rays incident normally on face AB of a right-angled prism ABC with 45 degree angle at C](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q180_013fe3aa_28102026.png?v=013fe3aa5b0c)

*Blue, green and red rays incident normally on face AB of a right-angled prism ABC with 45 degree angle at C*

- **(1)** Separate the red colour part from the green and blue colours
- (2) Separate the blue colour part from the red and green colours
- (3) Separate all the three colours from one another
- (4) Not separate the three colours at all

**Answer** — booklet: (1) · independent solve: (1) · agree

**Publisher's solution**

![printed figure: A horizontal ray incident at i = 45° on the hypotenuse face of a right-angled 45° prism](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/figures_agent/q180_sol_1_7d1a96ea_28102026.png?v=7d1a96eaeee3)

*A horizontal ray incident at i = 45° on the hypotenuse face of a right-angled 45° prism*

1. For TIR $I > I_{c}$ so $\mathrm{Sin}\ i > \sin I_{c}$
2. $\mathrm{Sin}\ 45^{\circ} > \frac{1}{\mu} \Rightarrow \mu\sqrt{2} \Rightarrow \mu = 1.414$
3. Since $\mu$ of green and violet are greater than $1.414$ so they will total internal refrected. But red colour will be vetracted.
4. So Ans. is (1)

**Solved independently** (answer withheld)

1. The beam is incident normally on face $AB$, so it enters undeviated.
2. From the geometry ($\angle C = 45^\circ$, $\angle B = 90^\circ$), the ray meets face $AC$ at an angle of incidence $i = 45^\circ$.
3. Total internal reflection at $AC$ requires $\sin 45^\circ > \frac{1}{n}$, i.e. $n > \sqrt{2} \approx 1.414$.
4. Red: $n = 1.39 < 1.414$, so red refracts out through $AC$.
5. Green: $n = 1.44 > 1.414$ and blue: $n = 1.47 > 1.414$, so both are totally internally reflected.
6. Hence red is separated from green and blue, which is option $1$.

**Subtopic:** Total Internal Reflection (TIR) · **Difficulty:** medium (3 steps)

**Printed in the booklet** (page 56)

![printed question 180](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/crops/q180_d54816e8_28102026.png?v=d54816e8099e)

Publisher's solution, as printed:

![printed solution 180](https://d30lmobgrc5gb8.cloudfront.net/general/28102026/neet_papers/2015/2015_A/solutions/q180_b71819f3_28102026.png?v=b71819f34281)

<!--ecat-->
#### Categories

- **Type:** **MCQ** (`mcq`) — a question and four answers, nothing lettered or listed
- **Asks:** **Rule applied** (`apply_rule`) — a rule you already know, used on a case you have not seen
- **Concept:** Total internal reflection at a 45 degree hypotenuse: a colour gets out only if its critical angle exceeds 45 degrees, that is only if $n<\sqrt{2}=1.414$
- **Trap:** **Reversed** (`reversed`) — the right thing stated backwards: direction, order or ratio — option 2  
  Carrying the index-to-critical-angle link the wrong way round, so that the largest $n$ is taken to give the largest critical angle, makes blue the colour that passes out of the hypotenuse; $\sin C=\frac{1}{n}$ gives blue the smallest critical angle, $42.9^\circ$, so blue is in fact the one reflected.
- **Why:** This question requires the critical-angle condition applied to each colour at the 45 degree hypotenuse, demonstrated by the stem printing three indices, "1.39, 1.44 and 1.47 respectively", and by the options sorting the colours into groups instead of naming any deviation or quantity.
- **Note:** The pattern classifier returned statement_options for this question. The stem "The prism will:" prints no statement block and no lettered list, and the long outcome sentences in the options are one event's possible results rather than claims to be graded, so the shape recorded here is mcq. The 45 degree incidence is not in the words of the stem; it comes from the figure, which the packet supplies as a printed PNG and as a spec with the right angle at B, 45 degrees at C and the three rays normal to AB, so the question is complete enough to tag.
<!--/ecat-->
